<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

For a positive integer $n$, let $j(n)$ be the least positive integer $m$ such that every interval of $m$ consecutive integers contains an integer coprime to $n$. Write $\omega(n)$ for the number of distinct prime divisors of $n$, with $\omega(1)=0$, and put $$h(k)=\sup_{\substack{n\ge1\\\omega(n)\le k}}j(n),\qquad k\ge1.$$ The starting point of the interval is arbitrary, and replacing $n$ by the product of its distinct prime divisors does not change $j(n)$. Thus $h(k)-1$ is the greatest length of an interval that can be covered by the divisibility classes of at most $k$ primes.

Jacobsthal studied this function in a series of papers beginning in 1960 (Jacobsthal 1960) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Jacobsthal1960](#ref-Jacobsthal1960)]. Erdős subsequently recorded Jacobsthal’s question whether $h(k)\ll k^2$ (Erdős 1962, 163, equations (1) and (3)) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Erdos1962](#ref-Erdos1962)]. In Erdős’s notation, $C(k)+1$ is the maximum of $j(n)$ over integers with exactly $k$ distinct prime divisors. This equals our $h(k)$: adjoining prime divisors cannot decrease $j(n)$. We prove the following bound, which gives an affirmative answer and a further iterated-logarithmic saving.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is an absolute constant $C>0$ such that $$h(k)\le C\frac{k^2}{(\log\log(3k))^2}
 \qquad\text{for every integer }k\ge1.$$*

</div>

All logarithms are natural. In particular the theorem gives a uniform quadratic bound for arbitrary sets of prime divisors, with no restriction on their sizes or on the interval’s translation.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context and related work

The development of the upper bound is closely tied to sieve theory. Erdős observed that Brun’s method gives $h(k)\ll k^{C_0}$ for some absolute exponent $C_0$ (Erdős 1962, 163) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Erdos1962](#ref-Erdos1962)]. Iwaniec’s work on the error term in the linear sieve gave an estimate of order $k^2\log^2 k$ when the prime divisors are the first $k$ primes (Iwaniec 1971) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Iwaniec1971](#ref-Iwaniec1971)]; this primorial case is distinguished explicitly in (R. C. Vaughan 1977, 329) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Vaughan1977](#ref-Vaughan1977)] and (Iwaniec 1978, 225–26) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Iwaniec1978](#ref-Iwaniec1978)]. Vaughan then proved the uniform estimate $$j(n)\ll\omega(n)^2\bigl(\log(2\omega(n))\bigr)^4
 \qquad(n>1)$$ (R. C. Vaughan 1977, Theorem, p. 329) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Vaughan1977](#ref-Vaughan1977)]. Iwaniec’s shifted-sieve argument removed two logarithmic factors and established $h(k)\ll k^2\log^2 k$ for arbitrary prime sets (Iwaniec 1978, Theorem and Corollary, p. 226) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Iwaniec1978](#ref-Iwaniec1978)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> improves this bound and, in particular, reaches the quadratic scale asked for by Jacobsthal.

Vaughan explained the exponent $2$ through the one-dimensional sieve’s restriction to moduli near the square root of the interval length (R. C. Vaughan 1977, 329) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Vaughan1977](#ref-Vaughan1977)]. In the usual linear-sieve notation this appears as the vanishing of the lower function $f$ at sieve parameter $2$. Our proof also reaches this limiting parameter. It keeps a boundary contribution that disappears from the leading linear-sieve term, and then controls the discrepancy between this reference calculation and the actual prescribed classes. The latter comparison requires the inverse estimate and the stopped counts developed below; positivity of the reference calculation alone does not give a lower bound for the original sieve.

A separate question concerns which prime sets give the largest gap. Let $p_k$ denote the $k$th prime and $P_k=\prod_{i=1}^k p_i$. Jacobsthal suggested that $j(P_k)=h(k)$ for every $k$. Hajdu and Saradha proved equality for $k\le23$, but found $$j(P_{24})=234<236=h(24)$$ (Hajdu and Saradha 2012, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[HajduSaradha2012](#ref-HajduSaradha2012)]. Thus the primorial extremality conjecture is distinct from the quadratic-bound question, and a theorem only about $j(P_k)$ does not settle the uniform problem. The computational literature reflects this distinction. Costello and Watts obtained recursive lower bounds for the minimum number of integers coprime to $P_k$ in an interval, and used them to compute upper bounds for $j(P_k)$ (Costello and Watts 2015, Theorem 4.4 and Section 5) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CostelloWatts2015](#ref-CostelloWatts2015)]. Ziller computed the maximum over arbitrary prime sets through $43$ prime factors (Ziller 2019, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ziller2019](#ref-Ziller2019)]. These finite computations concern explicit values, whereas the constant in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is not made explicit.

<a id="eq:historical-lower-bound"></a>
Primorials nevertheless give the strongest lower bounds relevant to the order of $h(k)$. They arise from constructions of long covered intervals, which also produce large gaps between consecutive primes. Erdős’s account already places Rankin’s lower-bound construction beside Brun’s polynomial upper bound (Erdős 1962, 163, equation (2)) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Erdos1962](#ref-Erdos1962)]. The later construction of Ford, Green, Konyagin, Maynard and Tao gives $$\label{eq:historical-lower-bound}
 j(P_k)\gg
 k(\log k)^2\frac{\log\log\log k}{\log\log k}
 \qquad(k\longrightarrow\infty)$$ (Ford et al. 2018, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[FGKMT2018](#ref-FGKMT2018)]. Indeed, their covered-interval bound at prime cutoff $x$ is $$\gg x\log x\,\log\log\log x/\log\log x;$$ take $x=p_k$ and use $p_k\sim k\log k$. Since $j(P_k)\le h(k)$, this supplies a lower bound for the uniform maximum as well. It is an existence construction and does not assert the same lower bound for every integer with $k$ prime divisors. The gap between <a href="#eq:historical-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:historical-lower-bound">[eq:historical-lower-bound]</a> and our upper bound remains substantial. Vaughan suggested the stronger possible estimate $j(n)\ll_\varepsilon\omega(n)^{1+\varepsilon}$ for $n>1$ and each fixed $\varepsilon>0$ (R. C. Vaughan 1977, 329) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Vaughan1977](#ref-Vaughan1977)].

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### A quantitative covering estimate

The proof works with one prescribed residue class at each prime below a size cutoff. This is another form of the same covering problem: for a finite prime set, the Chinese remainder theorem supplies an integer $b$ with $b\equiv a_p\pmod p$ at every prime in the set, and $$n\not\equiv a_p\pmod p\ \text{for every such }p
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \gcd\left(n-b,\prod p\right)=1.$$ This equivalence explains why arbitrary classes and arbitrary translations occur together; see also (Hajdu and Saradha 2012, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HajduSaradha2012](#ref-HajduSaradha2012)].

<a id="eq:parameters"></a>
For sufficiently large real $z$, set $$\label{eq:parameters}
\begin{gathered}
 L=\log z,\qquad Y=\left\lfloor\frac{z^2}{L^2}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad w=\frac{L}{(\log L)^2},\qquad B=\frac{L}{\log w},\\
 V_0=\prod_{p\le w}\left(1-\frac1p\right).
\end{gathered}$$ Products indexed by a letter explicitly called prime always range over primes. The two cutoffs separate the small primes, which are sieved inside arithmetic progressions, from the primes in $(w,z]$, which index the expansion used in the proof. The quantity $B$ is the logarithmic size of the upper cutoff in base $w$.

<a id="stmt-thm-survivors"></a>
<div id="thm:survivors" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $z_0$ with the following property. For every real $z\ge z_0$ and every choice of a residue class $a_p\pmod p$ for each prime $p\le z$, <a id="eq:main-survivors"></a>$$\label{eq:main-survivors}
 \#\{1\le n\le Y:n\not\equiv a_p\pmod p\text{ for every }p\le z\}
 \ge c\frac{YV_0}{B^2}.$$*

</div>

<a id="eq:survivor-scale"></a>
Mertens’ theorem identifies the size of this lower bound: $$\label{eq:survivor-scale}
 \frac{YV_0}{B^2}\sim e^{-\gamma}\frac{Y\log w}{L^2}.$$ The number of survivors, rather than positivity alone, permits the passage from a prime cutoff to a bound in terms of the number of prime divisors. For sufficiently large $k$, if $n$ has at most $k$ distinct prime divisors, apply Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a> at a cutoff $z=A_0 k\log k/\log\log k$, where $A_0$ is a sufficiently large fixed constant. A progression upper sieve bounds the total number of these survivors removed by the remaining divisor primes $q>z$ by $O(k(1+X/\log X))$, where $X=Y/z$. The lower bound in <a href="#eq:survivor-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:survivor-scale">[eq:survivor-scale]</a> dominates this loss for that choice of $A_0$, while $Y\asymp k^2/(\log\log k)^2$. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">11</a> gives the comparison with constants and handles the bounded range of $k$.

The quantitative estimate also relates to the interval-sieve quantity studied by Banks, Ford and Tao (Banks, Ford, and Tao 2023, secs. 1.3–1.4) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BanksFordTao2023](#ref-BanksFordTao2023)]. They minimize the survivor count over all choices of forbidden classes at primes up to $(y/\log y)^{1/2}$ in an interval of length $y$. At that cutoff a classical lower sieve gives a bound of order $y\log\log y/(\log y)^2$. Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a> retains this order of magnitude at the larger cutoff $z\asymp\sqrt Y\log Y$. The classes here are arbitrary; no random choice of the forbidden classes is assumed in this theorem.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Outline of the argument

The proof has two parts: a reference calculation for a decreasing-prime expansion, and a comparison with the actual residue classes. Section <a href="#sec:tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tree">3</a> partitions discarded integers by the least prime at which they hit a forbidden class. Iteration gives tuples of strictly decreasing primes. At even depths the expansion is a lower bound, and at odd depths it is an upper bound. An admission rule limits the progressions retained in this expansion so that each is long enough for an absolute upper sieve. Replacing its small-prime count by progression coordinate length times $V_0$ defines the reference tree.

The leading reference term is governed by the classical linear-sieve functions $f,F$ (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan, n.d.a, sec. 21.2.2), with the root tending to the critical parameter $2$. The lower exponent cutoff leaves a boundary contribution on the smaller scale $B^{-2}$. Each prime path has weight equal to the reciprocal product of its primes. Replacing reciprocal-prime sums by harmonic integrals $dx/x$ gives a continuous reference process. To evaluate the boundary contribution, Section <a href="#sec:functions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:functions">4</a> uses derivative weights of $f,F$ to normalize its transitions to a probability kernel. Its regeneration cycles give limiting occupation measures, which Section <a href="#sec:prime-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prime-paths">5</a> transfers to prime tuples. Section <a href="#sec:reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reference">6</a> then evaluates the boundary contribution and bounds the losses imposed by the admission rule. A positive margin remains after these losses and the negative contribution from the root’s small displacement below parameter $2$.

The actual small-prime counts agree with their reference values in long progressions, by the fundamental lemma of sieve theory. At a short progression, a large relative discrepancy must already be visible along one of the last few edges of its path. Section <a href="#sec:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:boxes">7</a> places these paths in boxes in which selected prime factors vary independently. An inverse estimate in Section <a href="#sec:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inverse">8</a> shows that many discrepant edges force alignment with one of finitely many rationals $A/D$: a prime $p$ aligns when $Da_p\equiv A\pmod p$.

The inverse step is related to the principle that modular concentration forces algebraic structure, developed by Helfgott and Venkatesh and by Walsh (Helfgott and Venkatesh 2009; Walsh 2012) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HelfgottVenkatesh2009](#ref-HelfgottVenkatesh2009), [Walsh2012](#ref-Walsh2012)]. Our argument uses partial incidence constraints in a single prime bin and obtains a rational list fixed for the whole box. It combines polynomial interpolation with Jarník’s convex-arc lattice-point argument (Jarník 1926) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Jarnik1926](#ref-Jarnik1926)] and the pair-counting principle of Gallagher’s larger sieve (Gallagher 1971) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Gallagher1971](#ref-Gallagher1971)]. Variance estimates in Section <a href="#sec:variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:variance">9</a> then show that a rational can account for many discrepant endpoints only when its effective modulus and intercept are small. The pair-moment calculation is a truncated form of mean singular-series averaging (Gallagher 1976) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Gallagher1976](#ref-Gallagher1976)]; the paper proves the required uniformity and stability under the changes of measure used here.

For the endpoints remaining after this argument, many earlier prime factors align with the same rational. Section <a href="#sec:stops" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stops">10</a> stops their branches at suitable even nodes. The stop rule depends on prime bins in a way that permits one aligned prime to vary over a large subset of its bin. Averaging over that prime gives more exact survivors than the reference subtree requires. A marked-renewal argument shows that all but an arbitrarily small amount of the exceptional path mass reaches one of these stops.

The comparison identity <a href="#eq:stopped-comparison-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stopped-comparison-identity">[eq:stopped-comparison-identity]</a> joins these parts. Proposition <a href="#prop:reference-margin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:reference-margin">22</a> supplies a positive reference term; the inverse and variance estimates leave only the endpoints of Corollary <a href="#cor:hard-endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:hard-endpoints">33</a>; and Propositions <a href="#prop:stopped-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stopped-comparison">38</a> and <a href="#prop:hard-stopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hard-stopping">39</a> supply the stopped comparison and its coverage. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">11</a> bounds the total remaining discrepancy, proves Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a>, and deduces Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Conventions

The constants in the proof are absolute after the explicitly stated parameters have been chosen. They need not be effective. Whenever a bin width $\xi$ occurs, it is fixed before $z$ tends to infinity. Constants independent of sufficiently small fixed $\xi$ do not assert uniformity for a width $\xi(z)$ tending to zero. This order matters in the rational-list and stopping arguments.

We use $\varphi$ for Euler’s totient, $\tau$ for the divisor function, $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(m)$ for the product of the distinct prime divisors of $m$, and $\bar u$ for a multiplicative inverse in a displayed modulus. All rationals $A/D$ used for alignment are reduced, with $D>0$.

<a id="sec:inputs"></a>
### Sieve and prime-distribution inputs

We state the forms of the classical estimates used in the proof. Appendix <a href="#sec:elementary-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elementary-inputs">13</a> supplies elementary proofs of the Buchstab normalization, the required additive large-sieve bound and the plane-curve intersection estimate, and derives the progression upper bound from the fundamental lemma. The inverse and stopping arguments in the later sections are proved directly.

<a id="stmt-lem-fundamental"></a>
<div id="lem:fundamental" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fundamental-input"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Fundamental lemma of the sieve). *Let $\mathcal P$ be any subset of the primes at most $v$. Suppose that intersection counts for bad conditions indexed by squarefree products $l$ of primes in $\mathcal P$ have the form $$Xg(l)+r_l,$$ where $g$ is multiplicative and $$0\le g(p)\le\frac2p,\qquad g(p)<1,\qquad g(2)\le\frac12
 \quad\text{if }2\in\mathcal P.$$ There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ and $s_0$ such that for $s\ge s_0$ the count avoiding all the bad conditions is $$\label{eq:fundamental-input}
 X\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}(1-g(p))\bigl(1+O(e^{-cs})\bigr)
 +O\!\left(
 \sum_{\substack{l\le v^s\\l\text{ squarefree}\\p\mid l\Rightarrow p\in\mathcal P}}
 \tau(l)^C|r_l|\right).$$ The same statement holds for nonnegative weighted counts. A local condition that makes the sifted set empty may instead be removed from consideration as an exactly empty case.*

</div>

This is a standard dimension-two form of the fundamental lemma; see (Sofos 2023, Lemma 2.8, p. S137) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Sofos2023](#ref-Sofos2023)], which records the stronger remainder sum without divisor weights and attributes the result to (Friedlander and Iwaniec 2010, Corollary 6.10) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[FriedlanderIwaniec2010](#ref-FriedlanderIwaniec2010)]. To check the uniform dimension condition here, put $g(p)=0$ outside $\mathcal P$. For $p\ge3$, $$(1-2/p)^{-1}=(1-1/p)^{-2}\bigl(1+O(p^{-2})\bigr).$$ Mertens’ theorem therefore gives $$\prod_{u\le p<v}(1-g(p))^{-1}
 \le K\left(\frac{\log v}{\log u}\right)^2
 \qquad(2\le u<v)$$ with an absolute $K$. The factor at 2 is at most 2. The sieve weights depend only on intersections of the bad conditions, so their use is not restricted to literal divisibility conditions.

<a id="stmt-lem-prime-inputs"></a>
<div id="lem:prime-inputs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Prime-distribution estimates). *The following estimates will be used, with absolute constants unless fixed subscripts indicate a dependence.*

1.  *There is $c>0$ such that $$\pi(v)=\mathop{\mathrm{li}}(v)+O\!\left(\frac v{\log v}e^{-c\sqrt{\log v}}\right),
     \qquad
     \prod_{p\le v}(1-1/p)
     =\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{\log v}
       \bigl(1+O(e^{-c\sqrt{\log v}})\bigr).$$ Decreasing $c$ absorbs harmless powers of $\log v$.*

2.  *For every fixed $A,M>0$, uniformly for $q\le(\log v)^A$ and $(a,q)=1$, $$\pi(v;q,a)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{li}}(v)}{\varphi(q)}
                 +O_{A,M}\!\left(\frac v{(\log v)^M}\right).$$ The implied constants may be ineffective.*

3.  *For $(a,q)=1$ and $H/q$ exceeding a suitable absolute constant, $$\#\{x<p\le x+H:p\equiv a\pmod q\}
     \ll\frac{H}{\varphi(q)\log(H/q)}.$$*

4.  *Uniformly for $v^{9/10}\le H\le v$ as $v\to\infty$, $$\#\{v-H<p\le v\}\ge(1-o(1))\frac H{\log v}.$$*

</div>

Part (i) is the classical zero-free-region PNT together with its Mertens product consequence; see (Iwaniec and Kowalski 2004) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[IwaniecKowalski2004](#ref-IwaniecKowalski2004)], and the stronger explicit PNT error in (Fiori, Kadiri, and Swidinsky 2023) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[FioriKadiriSwidinsky2023](#ref-FioriKadiriSwidinsky2023)]. Parts (ii) and (iii) are Siegel–Walfisz and Brun–Titchmarsh; see the explicit statements in (Granville and Soundararajan, n.d., 75) and (Granville and Soundararajan 2014, Theorem 1.4.4, p. 39) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[GranvilleSoundararajan2014](#ref-GranvilleSoundararajan2014)], respectively. Lemma <a href="#lem:elementary-brun-titchmarsh" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:elementary-brun-titchmarsh">45</a> derives the order bound in part (iii) from the stated sieve input and Mertens’ formula. Part (iv) follows from the short-interval PNT of Huxley, for example in Heath-Brown’s proof (Heath-Brown 1982, Theorem, p. 1365) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[HeathBrown1982](#ref-HeathBrown1982)]. To see that a fixed-exponent version suffices, tile an interval of length at least $v^{9/10}$ by intervals ending at $x$ of length $x^{4/5}$. Stop before the final incomplete tile or before $x<v^{1/2}$. The lost total length is $O(v^{4/5}+v^{1/2})=o(H)$. All remaining endpoints tend to infinity uniformly, so the fixed-exponent theorem sums over the tiles. Prime powers contribute a negligible amount, and $\log p\le\log v$ gives the stated prime-count lower bound.

<a id="eq:large-sieve-input"></a>
We also use the additive large sieve in the following standard form: for distinct reduced fractions with denominators at most $Q$, and coefficients supported on $J$ consecutive integers, $$\label{eq:large-sieve-input}
 \sum_{q\le Q}\ \sum_{\substack{a\bmod q\\(a,q)=1}}
 \left|\sum_j b_j e(aj/q)\right|^2
 \ll(J+Q^2)\sum_j|b_j|^2,
 \qquad e(t)=e^{2\pi it}.$$ See (H. L. Montgomery and Vaughan 1973) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan1973](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan1973)] and (Robert C. Vaughan 2025, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[VaughanLargeSieve](#ref-VaughanLargeSieve)]. Lemma <a href="#lem:elementary-large-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:elementary-large-sieve">43</a> proves the order bound needed here.

<a id="eq:kloosterman-input"></a>
For the complete Kloosterman sum $$\mathop{\mathrm{Kl}}(h,k;N)=\sum_{\substack{u\bmod N\\(u,N)=1}}
 e\!\left(\frac{hu+k\bar u}{N}\right)$$ we use $$\label{eq:kloosterman-input}
 |\mathop{\mathrm{Kl}}(h,k;N)|\ll \tau(N)(h,k,N)^{1/2}N^{1/2}.$$ This holds for arbitrary positive moduli, including prime powers, and for noncoprime or zero frequencies; see (Lichtman 2022, Lemma 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Lichtman2022](#ref-Lichtman2022)] and (Iwaniec and Kowalski 2004, Corollary 11.12, p. 280) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[IwaniecKowalski2004](#ref-IwaniecKowalski2004)]. The gcd factor in <a href="#eq:kloosterman-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kloosterman-input">[eq:kloosterman-input]</a> will be retained in all completions.

<a id="eq:renewal-input"></a>
The probability input is the nonarithmetic iid key renewal theorem: if positive iid increments have finite positive mean $\mu$, renewal measure $U$, and $H$ is directly Riemann integrable, then $$\label{eq:renewal-input}
 (U*H)(v)\longrightarrow\frac1\mu\int_\mathbb RH(u)\,du.$$ We apply the usual half-line theorem after translating tests that vanish on a left half-line; see (Serfozo 2009, sec. 2.7, Theorem 35) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Serfozo2009](#ref-Serfozo2009)]. The regeneration, finite-mean, nonarithmetic and direct-integrability hypotheses needed here are proved in Section <a href="#sec:functions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:functions">4</a>. There we also use the classical Buchstab limit $\omega_{\mathrm{Bu}}(s)\to e^{-\gamma}$, with its independent local proof in Lemma <a href="#lem:elementary-buchstab" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:elementary-buchstab">42</a>; see also (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan, n.d.b, sec. 28.9, p. 236).

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Small-prime counts in a progression

<a id="stmt-lem-small-sieve"></a>
<div id="lem:small-sieve" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:small-upper"></a>
<a id="eq:small-relative"></a>
**Lemma 5**. *Let one forbidden class be given at every prime $p\le u$, where $u\to\infty$. Count the points in a progression segment whose coordinate interval has real length $J$, and whose step is coprime to every prime $p\le u$. Put $V_{\mathrm{sm}}(u)=\prod_{p\le u}(1-1/p)$. For each fixed $\eta>0$, uniformly when $J\ge u^\eta$, its sifted count $N$ satisfies $$\label{eq:small-upper}
 N\ll_\eta JV_{\mathrm{sm}}(u).$$ There are absolute $c_1,C_1>0$ and a sufficiently large fixed $v_0$ such that, writing $v=\log_uJ$, uniformly for $v\ge v_0$, $$\label{eq:small-relative}
 \left|\frac{N}{JV_{\mathrm{sm}}(u)}-1\right|\le C_1e^{-c_1v}.$$ All estimates are independent of the progression’s initial point, step and forbidden classes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-small-sieve"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-small-sieve</summary>

*Proof.* For any squarefree $l$ supported on the small primes, the simultaneous bad conditions specify one class of the progression coordinate modulo $l$. Their count is $J/l+O(1)$, with an absolute error independent of the step and the classes. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:fundamental" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fundamental">3</a> at level $J^{1/2}$. Its sieve parameter is $v/2$, and the remainder sum is $$\ll J^{1/2}(1+\log J)^{C_2}$$ for an absolute fixed $C_2$. After division by $JV_{\mathrm{sm}}(u)$, this is $$\ll J^{-1/2}(1+\log J)^{C_2}\log u.$$ For all sufficiently large $u$ this is at most $e^{-c_1v}$ uniformly for $v\ge v_0$, on decreasing $c_1$ and increasing $v_0$ if necessary. The relative fundamental-lemma error has the same form. This proves <a href="#eq:small-relative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-relative">[eq:small-relative]</a>.

For <a href="#eq:small-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-upper">[eq:small-upper]</a>, sieve only by primes at most $u^\theta$, where $0<\theta<1$ is fixed sufficiently small in terms of $\eta$. At level $J^{1/2}$ the sieve parameter is at least $\eta/(2\theta)$ and can therefore be made larger than $s_0$. The upper fundamental lemma gives $$N\ll JV_{\mathrm{sm}}(u^\theta)+J^{1/2}(1+\log J)^{C_2}
 \ll_\eta JV_{\mathrm{sm}}(u),$$ using Mertens and $J\ge u^\eta$. The same calculation covers either endpoint convention and nonintegral $J$, since each intersection still has an $O(1)$ endpoint error. ◻

</details>

We will repeatedly apply <a href="#eq:small-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-upper">[eq:small-upper]</a> to short difference pieces created by freezing an interval length. If $\xi>0$ is fixed and the coordinate length of such a piece is comparable to $\xi J$, with $\xi J\ge u^\eta$, its cost is $O_\eta(\xi JV_{\mathrm{sm}}(u))$. The constant does not depend on $\xi$; the threshold from which the length inequality holds may depend on that fixed width. The same observation applies to a specified residue subsequence of the piece.

<a id="sec:tree"></a>
### The decreasing-prime tree

Fix the residue classes in Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a>, and use <a href="#eq:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameters">[eq:parameters]</a>. Set $$a_\star=\frac1{100},\qquad x(p)=\frac{\log p}{\log w}.$$ Thus $x(p)$ measures a prime’s exponent in base $w$, and $1<x(p)\le B$ for $p\in(w,z]$. A node is a finite tuple of strictly decreasing primes in $(w,z]$. Its product is denoted by $d$, with $d=1$ for the empty tuple. Put <a id="eq:tree-state"></a>$$\label{eq:tree-state}
 J_d=\frac Yd,\qquad
 r_d=\log_wJ_d-a_\star+2,\qquad \mu_d=J_dV_0.$$ At the root, $r_0=2B-a_\star+o(1)$ and the cutoff is $b=B$. At a nonempty node, the cutoff is $b=x(p_{\mathrm{last}})$, and the available primes satisfy $w<p<p_{\mathrm{last}}$. The root cutoff includes $p=z$ when $z$ is prime. All subsequent cutoffs are strict.

Let $N_d$ count integers $1\le n\le Y$ that hit every prescribed class at the prime factors of $d$ and avoid every prescribed class at the primes at most $w$. Let $S_d(b)$ impose, in addition, avoidance at all available primes above $w$ up to the cutoff. In particular, the survivor count in Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a> is $S_1(B)$. The simultaneous hits give one class modulo $d$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:small-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:small-sieve">5</a> applies in its progression coordinates.

<a id="eq:tree-identity"></a>
Partition the integers counted by $N_d$ but not by $S_d(b)$ according to their smallest bad available prime. This gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:tree-identity}
 S_d(b)=N_d-\sum_{p\text{ available}}S_{dp}(x(p)).$$ Although the partition uses the smallest bad prime, iteration produces decreasing prime tuples because the child count avoids all smaller available primes.

<a id="eq:admission"></a>
Nodes of even length are called lower nodes, and nodes of odd length are called upper nodes. From a lower node include every available child. From an upper node include a child with exponent $x=x(p)$ exactly when $$\label{eq:admission}
 r-x\ge \mathcal H(x),\qquad \mathcal H(x)=\max(2x,x+2),$$ where $r$ is the parent’s gap. Other children are omitted.

<a id="stmt-lem-tree-lower-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:tree-lower-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *The alternating sum of the $N_d$ over all included nodes is a lower bound for $S_1(B)$. More generally, one may stop at any prefix-free collection of included lower nodes, use their exact counts $S_d(b)$, and expand only the other nodes. The resulting expression is still a lower bound for $S_1(B)$. Every included node satisfies $J_d\ge w^{a_\star}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tree-lower-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tree-lower-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The tree is finite because all factors are distinct primes at most $z$. Work upward from its leaves. At a lower node, replacing each child by an upper bound in <a href="#eq:tree-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-identity">[eq:tree-identity]</a> gives a lower bound. At an upper node, omitting nonnegative child counts and replacing the retained children by lower bounds gives an upper bound. This proves the sign assertion by induction. An exact count at a lower node is an admissible lower bound there; prefix-freeness ensures that its descendants are not expanded as well.

At a nonroot lower node, admission gives $r\ge2b$ and $r\ge b+2$. Every child exponent satisfies $1<x\le b$, so its gap is at least 2. At the root, $r_0-B=B-a_\star+o(1)>2$ for large $z$, giving the same conclusion for every first child. Included lower nodes also have gap exceeding 2 by their admission rule. Hence all included nodes have $\log_wJ_d=r_d-2+a_\star\ge a_\star$. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#fig:admission-and-stopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:admission-and-stopping">1</a> separates the admission decision at an upper node from the later choice of lower stops.

<figure id="fig:admission-and-stopping">
<div class="picture">

At the upper node, admission is the test $r_{dp} − x(v) ≥ ℋ(x(v))$ for the prospective prime $v$. The omitted child $dpu$ does not belong to the included tree. The admitted child $dpq$ is shown as a member of a separately chosen, prefix-free set of lower stops; its descendants are replaced by its exact survivor count. Other included lower nodes continue to expand. The specific stop rule is given in Section <a href="#sec:stops" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stops">10</a>.</figcaption>

<a id="eq:tree-small-counts"></a>
In particular, uniformly at included nodes, $$\label{eq:tree-small-counts}
 \frac{N_d}{\mu_d}\ll_{a_\star}1,
 \qquad
 \left|\frac{N_d}{\mu_d}-1\right|
 \ll e^{-c_1\log_wJ_d}
 \quad\text{when }\log_wJ_d\ge v_0.$$ The absolute upper bound remains valid on the slightly enlarged progressions with $J_d\ge w^{a_\star/2}$ that will arise in boxes.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The reference tree

Replace $N_d$ by $\mu_d$ in the unstopped tree. Divide a subtree value by its own $\mu_d$, and denote the result by $P_\mathrm e(r,b)$ or $P_\mathrm o(r,b)$ according to parity. Dependence on $w$ and on the endpoint convention is left implicit. Since $\mu_{dp}=\mu_d/p$, the recursions are <a id="eq:reference-tree"></a>$$\label{eq:reference-tree}
\begin{split}
 P_\mathrm e(r,b)&=1-\sum_{p\text{ available}}\frac1p
                         P_\mathrm o(r-x(p),x(p)),\\
 P_\mathrm o(r,b)&=1-
 \sum_{\substack{p\text{ available}\\r-x(p)\ge \mathcal H(x(p))}}
 \frac1p P_\mathrm e(r-x(p),x(p)).
\end{split}$$ The reference value at the root is $YV_0P_\mathrm e(r_0,B)$. Auxiliary starting states used below follow the same recursions on their declared domains; no actual forbidden classes are needed to define them.

A path’s *harmonic weight* is the reciprocal product of its chosen primes. Dropping all admission restrictions shows that the sum of the weights of all decreasing-prime prefixes is <a id="eq:total-prefix-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:total-prefix-mass}
 \prod_{w<p\le z}\left(1+\frac1p\right)\ll B.$$ Indeed $\log(1+1/p)=1/p+O(p^{-2})$, and the reciprocal-prime sum is $\log B+O(1)$.

We also use *standard* paths: these keep only the admission condition $r-x\ge2x$ at an upper node. They contain all paths of the original tree and agree with it whenever the next exponent is at least 2. The continuous harmonic model replaces reciprocal-prime sums by $dx/x$, with decreasing real exponents. It will be studied first, then compared with the actual prime paths.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Comparison after stopping

<a id="eq:stopped-comparison-identity"></a>
For later use, the comparison identity can be recorded explicitly. Let $\mathcal T$ be the retained expanded nodes of a stopped tree, excluding the stop nodes, and let $\mathcal S$ be its prefix-free set of lower stops. If $L_{\mathrm{stop}}$ is its lower-bound value, then $$\label{eq:stopped-comparison-identity}
\begin{split}
 L_{\mathrm{stop}}-YV_0P_\mathrm e(r_0,B)
 ={}&\sum_{d\in\mathcal T}(-1)^{\omega(d)}(N_d-\mu_d)\\
 &+\sum_{d\in\mathcal S}
 \bigl(S_d(b_d)-\mu_dP_\mathrm e(r_d,b_d)\bigr).
\end{split}$$ Here a node is identified by its decreasing tuple, equivalently by its squarefree product. To verify the identity, expand the full reference tree down to the same stops; at each stop its remaining subtree is exactly $\mu_dP_\mathrm e(r_d,b_d)$. Thus a positive reference margin, a small sum of retained absolute errors, and an aggregate nonnegative stopped difference will prove Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a>.

<a id="sec:functions"></a>
### Delay functions and the continuous path process

We first analyze the continuous model in which a prime exponent is chosen with harmonic measure $dx/x$. The linear-sieve functions provide its benchmark, and their derivatives will convert harmonic path weights into probability weights. We then use regeneration to evaluate compact-endpoint sums at scale $B^{-2}$ and to control their tails. These are the estimates needed to propagate the reference tree’s boundary discrepancy.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Future integrals for the sieve functions

We use the classical lower and upper linear-sieve functions; see (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan, n.d.a, sec. 21.2.2). The future-integral and path representations needed below are developed here.

<a id="eq:functions-starting-extension"></a>
Write $A=2e^\gamma$. Define the continuous delay functions $f,F$ by $$f(s)=0\quad(0\le s\le2),\qquad sF(s)=A\quad(1\le s\le3),$$ and $$(sf(s))'=F(s-1)\quad(s>2),\qquad
 (sF(s))'=f(s-1)\quad(s>3).$$ These prescriptions determine the functions successively on intervals of length one. We shall also use $F(s)=A/s$ on $0.95\le s<1$. Only at a starting lower state, and when defining its derivative weight, we continue $f$ below $2$ by $$\label{eq:functions-starting-extension}
 f_{\rm ext}(s)=\frac{A\log(s-1)}s\qquad(1.98\le s<2).$$ This is the same expression as the ordinary $f$ on $2\le s\le4$. An omitted lower child of ratio less than $2$ always has benchmark $f=0$; the continuation <a href="#eq:functions-starting-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-starting-extension">[eq:functions-starting-extension]</a> is not used at such a child.

<a id="eq:functions-benchmark-cutoff"></a>
Put $f_\mathrm e=f$ and $f_\mathrm o=F$. The continuous benchmark at a state of type $i$, gap $r$, and cutoff $b$ is $f_i(r/b)/b$. For fixed $r$, the delay equations give $$\label{eq:functions-benchmark-cutoff}
 \frac{d}{db}\left(\frac{f_i(r/b)}b\right)
       =-\frac{f_{i'}(r/b-1)}{b^2},$$ where $i'$ is the opposite type. On the right, the value at an omitted lower child is defined to be zero, including when its formal ratio is negative. On the ordinary ranges the identity holds for $r/b>2$ at a lower state and $r/b>1$ at an upper state. At an upper state the right side is zero when $r/b-1\le2$, exactly as required for an omitted lower child. Integrating with respect to the cutoff therefore gives the standard continuous tree recurrence above exponent $2$, with the boundary value at cutoff $2$ left to be specified. The explicit starting extensions satisfy the same identity in the interiors of their ranges, with one-sided derivatives at the endpoints; the extension of $f$ is used at the parent lower state, never at an omitted child.

The root lies at $r_0/B=2-a_\star/B+o(B^{-1})$, where the ordinary lower-sieve function vanishes. Its continued benchmark is $$\frac{f_{\rm ext}(r_0/B)}B
       =-a_\star e^\gamma B^{-2}+o(B^{-2}),$$ since $f'_{\rm ext}(2)=e^\gamma$. Thus a positive reference margin must come from the finite boundary, after its discrepancy has been propagated through all larger exponents. To evaluate that contribution, we will use the positive derivative weights of $f,F$ to normalize the harmonic transitions. The first step is to establish positivity and decay in a form that justifies this change of measure.

Let $\rho$ be the Dickman function, determined by $$\rho(u)=1\quad(0\le u\le1),\qquad
 u\rho'(u)=-\rho(u-1)\quad(u>1).$$ For the unextended functions put $$a(s)=1-f(s)\quad(s\ge0),\qquad
 c(s)=F(s)-1\quad(s\ge1),\qquad
 c_0(s)=\frac{A\rho(s-1)}s\quad(s\ge1).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-future-integrals"></a>
<div id="lem:future-integrals" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:functions-deficits"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-future-c"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-dickman-ratio"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-dickman-logderivative"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Future integrals). *The functions $a,c$ are positive and satisfy, with absolute comparison constants, <a id="eq:functions-future-a"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 a(s)+c(s)&=c_0(s),&
 a(s)&\asymp c_0(s),&
 c(s)&\asymp c_0(s)\qquad(s\ge1),\label{eq:functions-deficits}\\
 s a(s)&=\int_{s-1}^{\infty}c(t)\,dt
       &&(s\ge2),\label{eq:functions-future-a}\\
 s c(s)&=\int_{s-1}^{\infty}a(t)\,dt
       &&(s\ge1).\label{eq:functions-future-c}
\end{aligned}$$ For $u\ge2$, $$\label{eq:functions-dickman-ratio}
 \frac1{4u^2}\le\frac{\rho(u)}{\rho(u-1)}\le\frac1u .$$ Moreover, for each fixed $\lambda>0$, there is $U_\lambda$ such that $$\label{eq:functions-dickman-logderivative}
 -\frac{\rho'(u)}{\rho(u)}\ge\lambda\qquad(u\ge U_\lambda).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-future-integrals"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-future-integrals</summary>

<a id="eq:functions-dickman-integral"></a>
*Proof.* The delay equation gives the integral identity $$\label{eq:functions-dickman-integral}
 u\rho(u)=\int_{u-1}^{u}\rho(v)\,dv\qquad(u\ge1).$$ For example, both sides agree at $u=1$, and their derivatives agree where the delay equation applies. The identity and the delay equation give positivity and monotonicity: a first zero of $\rho$ would contradict the positive integral of its immediately preceding values. Monotonicity gives the upper bound in <a href="#eq:functions-dickman-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-dickman-ratio">[eq:functions-dickman-ratio]</a>. Integrating first over the lower half of each of two unit intervals gives $$\rho(u)\ge\frac{\rho(u-\tfrac12)}{2u}
 \ge\frac{\rho(u-1)}{4u(u-\tfrac12)}
 \ge\frac{\rho(u-1)}{4u^2}.$$ The upper ratio bound also shows that $\rho$ tends to zero faster than any exponential.

The negative logarithmic derivative is at least $1$. If it is at least $\lambda$ on a tail, then, on a later tail, $$\int_{u-1}^{u}\rho(v)\,dv
 \le \rho(u-1)\frac{1-e^{-\lambda}}{\lambda}.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:functions-dickman-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-dickman-integral">[eq:functions-dickman-integral]</a> consequently improves the lower bound to $\lambda/(1-e^{-\lambda})$. Iteration tends to infinity, since this map is strictly larger than its argument and has no finite positive fixed point. This proves <a href="#eq:functions-dickman-logderivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-dickman-logderivative">[eq:functions-dickman-logderivative]</a>.

<a id="eq:functions-gap-sum"></a>
Directly from the delay equations and the initial conditions, $$\label{eq:functions-gap-sum}
 F(s)-f(s)=c_0(s),\qquad
 F(s)+f(s)=A\omega_{\rm Bu}(s)\qquad(s\ge1).$$ Here the continuous Buchstab function is determined by $s\omega_{\rm Bu}(s)=1$ on $[1,2]$ and $(s\omega_{\rm Bu}(s))'=\omega_{\rm Bu}(s-1)$ above $2$; its limit is stated in Section <a href="#sec:inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inputs">2</a>. For the first equality, the equation for $s(F-f)$ is the negative delay equation for $A\rho(s-1)$; for the second, it is the Buchstab equation with initial value $A/s$. We next prove the individual positivity and future integrals; these do not follow merely by taking the difference in <a href="#eq:functions-gap-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-gap-sum">[eq:functions-gap-sum]</a>.

To prove these properties, we use an auxiliary absorbing chain. This chain establishes positivity of the deficits; the harmonic-path chain will instead be defined from the derivative weights below. For $s\ge2$, give the auxiliary chain transition density <a id="eq:functions-auxiliary-kernel"></a>$$\label{eq:functions-auxiliary-kernel}
 \frac{c_0(t)}{s c_0(s)}\,\mathbf 1_{\{t\ge s-1\}}\,dt,$$ and absorb it on reaching $[1,2)$. Its normalization is $$\int_{s-1}^{\infty}c_0(t)\,dt
 =A\int_{s-2}^{\infty}\frac{\rho(v)}{v+1}\,dv
 =A\rho(s-1)=s c_0(s).$$ The integration at infinity is justified by the decay already proved. The excess of the next state above $s-1$ has tail $$\frac{\rho(s-1+h)}{\rho(s-1)}\qquad(h\ge0).$$ For large $s$, <a href="#eq:functions-dickman-logderivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-dickman-logderivative">[eq:functions-dickman-logderivative]</a> bounds this tail by $e^{-\lambda h}$, with $\lambda$ as large as desired. For $\lambda>1$, therefore, $$\mathbb E(e^{s_{\rm next}}\mid s)
 \le e^{s-1}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}.$$ Taking $\lambda=2$ makes this a strict contraction of $V(s)=e^s$, with factor $2/e<1$, outside a fixed compact. Stopped drift implies almost sure return to that compact. From any state in the compact, successive draws in $[s-1,s-1+1/4]$ reach $[1,2)$ within a bounded number of steps, with probability bounded below independently of the initial state in the compact. The densities in these intervals have a positive compact lower bound. Repeating the attempt after each return proves almost sure absorption.

Alternate labels $a,c$ at every draw. At the terminal state $t$ assign fractions $1/c_0(t)$ and $1-1/c_0(t)$ to the two labels, respectively. Since $c_0(t)=A/t$ on $[1,2)$, both terminal fractions lie in a fixed compact subinterval of $(0,1)$. The expected terminal fraction, multiplied by $c_0(s)$, defines positive functions $a^\circ(s),c^\circ(s)$ with $$a^\circ+c^\circ=c_0,\qquad
 a^\circ,c^\circ\asymp c_0,$$ and terminal values $a^\circ=1,\ c^\circ=c_0-1$ on $[1,2)$. The first-step equations give $$s a^\circ(s)=\int_{s-1}^{\infty}c^\circ(t)\,dt,\qquad
 s c^\circ(s)=\int_{s-1}^{\infty}a^\circ(t)\,dt
 \qquad(s\ge2).$$ In particular these functions are continuous from the right at $2$.

Set $U=(2+c^\circ-a^\circ)/A$. The last two equations imply $(sU(s))'=U(s-1)$ above $2$, in integrated form, while $sU(s)=1$ on $[1,2)$. Also $U(s)\to 2/A=e^{-\gamma}$, because $a^\circ,c^\circ$ are bounded by $c_0$. There can be no jump at $2$ relative to the continuous Buchstab solution. Indeed, a jump $\delta$ in $sU(s)$ there would give a difference $\delta H(s)$, where $H(s)=1/s$ on $[2,3]$ and $(sH(s))'=H(s-1)$ thereafter. The bound $H\ge1/3$ on $[2,3]$ persists by induction: if it holds up to an integer $n\ge3$, then for $n\le s\le n+1$, $$sH(s)=nH(n)+\int_n^s H(v-1)\,dv\ge n/3+(s-n)/3.$$ For either sign of $\delta\ne0$, this contradicts $U(s)-\omega_{\rm Bu}(s)\to0$, using the independently proved Buchstab limit in Lemma <a href="#lem:elementary-buchstab" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:elementary-buchstab">42</a>. Thus $U=\omega_{\rm Bu}$. Together with $a^\circ+c^\circ=c_0$ and <a href="#eq:functions-gap-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-gap-sum">[eq:functions-gap-sum]</a>, this identifies $a^\circ=a,\ c^\circ=c$, proving <a href="#eq:functions-deficits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-deficits">[eq:functions-deficits]</a> and both future integrals for $s\ge2$.

Finally $c(s)=A/s-1$ and $a=1$ below $2$. The equation at $s=2$ gives $\int_1^\infty a=A-2$; hence for $1\le s\le2$, $$\int_{s-1}^{\infty}a(t)\,dt=2-s+A-2=A-s=s c(s).$$ This proves the remaining range of <a href="#eq:functions-future-c" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-future-c">[eq:functions-future-c]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Derivative weights and change of measure

The deficits are now positive and have the required decay. We use derivatives of the sieve functions to normalize the harmonic transitions of the standard tree.

Define $$\phi_\mathrm e(s)=s^2f'(s)\quad(s\ge2),\qquad
 \phi_\mathrm o(s)=-s^2F'(s)\quad(s\ge0.95).$$ At $s=2$ use the right derivative, and on $1.98\le s<2$ use the derivative of <a href="#eq:functions-starting-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-starting-extension">[eq:functions-starting-extension]</a>. The state weight will be $\phi_i(r/b)/r^2$; its reciprocal at the endpoint is the factor that appears when we convert a probability estimate back to harmonic weight. For paths from the root, this gives the scale $B^{-2}$ when the endpoint gap and ratio remain in fixed compact ranges. Put $$W_0(t)=\frac{t+1}{t^2},\qquad
 m_\mathrm e(s)=s-1,\qquad m_\mathrm o(s)=\max(2,s-1).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-derivative-weights"></a>
<div id="lem:derivative-weights" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:functions-derivative-integrals"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-phi-dickman"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Derivative weights). *The derivative weights are positive and nonincreasing on their indicated ranges. If $i'$ denotes the type opposite to $i$, then $$\label{eq:functions-derivative-integrals}
 \phi_i(s)=\int_{m_i(s)}^{\infty}W_0(t)\phi_{i'}(t)\,dt.$$ This holds also on the specified starting extensions. On a tail, $$\label{eq:functions-phi-dickman}
 \phi_\mathrm e(s)\asymp\rho(s-2),\qquad
 \phi_\mathrm o(s)\asymp\rho(s-2).$$ There are absolute constants $C,q$ such that the absolute logarithmic derivatives, where they exist, and the ratios $\phi_{i'}(m_i(s))/\phi_i(s)$ are at most $C(1+s)^q$. In particular, for $u,v\le S$ in the indicated domain of the same weight, with $S\ge3$, $$|\log\phi_i(u)-\log\phi_i(v)|
 \le C(1+S)^q|u-v|.$$ For each fixed $\lambda>0$, the negative logarithmic derivatives of both weights are at least $\lambda$ on a sufficiently remote tail.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-derivative-weights"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-derivative-weights</summary>

<a id="eq:functions-phi-deficits"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-phi-e-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-phi-o-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* On the ordinary ranges the delay equations give $$\label{eq:functions-phi-deficits}
 \phi_\mathrm e(s)=s\bigl(a(s)+c(s-1)\bigr),\qquad
 \phi_\mathrm o(s)=s\bigl(c(s)+a(s-1)\bigr).$$ The second expression uses $a=1$ on $[0,1]$. On the extensions, the explicit formulas are $$\phi_\mathrm e(s)=A\left(\frac{s}{s-1}-\log(s-1)\right),\qquad
 \phi_\mathrm o(s)=A\quad(0.95\le s\le3).$$ These formulas and Lemma <a href="#lem:future-integrals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:future-integrals">7</a> prove positivity. Differentiation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \phi_\mathrm e'(s)&=-\frac{s}{(s-1)^2}\phi_\mathrm o(s-1)
       &&(s\ge1.98),\label{eq:functions-phi-e-derivative}\\
 \phi_\mathrm o'(s)&=-\frac{s}{(s-1)^2}\phi_\mathrm e(s-1)
       &&(s>3),\label{eq:functions-phi-o-derivative}
\end{aligned}$$ at points of differentiability; the odd weight is constant below $3$. Equation <a href="#eq:functions-phi-deficits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-deficits">[eq:functions-phi-deficits]</a> and <a href="#eq:functions-deficits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-deficits">[eq:functions-deficits]</a> give <a href="#eq:functions-phi-dickman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-dickman">[eq:functions-phi-dickman]</a>, and in particular both weights vanish at infinity. Integrating <a href="#eq:functions-phi-e-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-e-derivative">[eq:functions-phi-e-derivative]</a> and <a href="#eq:functions-phi-o-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-o-derivative">[eq:functions-phi-o-derivative]</a> backwards from infinity gives <a href="#eq:functions-derivative-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-derivative-integrals">[eq:functions-derivative-integrals]</a>. For the even extension, the lower endpoint lies below $1$, where the explicitly constant odd weight gives exactly the displayed continuation. For odd $s\le3$, the lower endpoint is $2$, so the integral is constant, as required.

On a tail, <a href="#eq:functions-dickman-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-dickman-ratio">[eq:functions-dickman-ratio]</a> and <a href="#eq:functions-phi-dickman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-dickman">[eq:functions-phi-dickman]</a> give $$\frac{\phi_{i'}(s-1)}{\phi_i(s)}=O(s^2).$$ The derivative formulas then bound the absolute logarithmic derivatives by a polynomial (in fact $O(s)$ on a tail). On the remaining compact ranges the explicit positive functions and their piecewise derivatives give fixed bounds. The weights are locally absolutely continuous by <a href="#eq:functions-derivative-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-derivative-integrals">[eq:functions-derivative-integrals]</a>; integrating the logarithmic derivative gives the stated Lipschitz bound. Finally the same comparison gives a positive constant times $$\frac{\rho(s-3)}{s\rho(s-2)}
 =\frac{s-2}{s}\left(-\frac{\rho'(s-2)}{\rho(s-2)}\right)$$ as a lower bound for either negative logarithmic derivative on a tail. Apply <a href="#eq:functions-dickman-logderivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-dickman-logderivative">[eq:functions-dickman-logderivative]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:tilted-kernel"></a>
A *standard* path replaces the admission condition <a href="#eq:admission" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:admission">[eq:admission]</a> by $r-x\ge2x$ at an upper node. Thus it contains every path in the original tree, and the two rules agree for $x\ge2$. At a state of type $i$, gap $r$, cutoff $b$, and ratio $s=r/b$, make a draw $t$ with probability density $$\label{eq:tilted-kernel}
 K_i(s,dt)=
 \frac{W_0(t)\phi_{i'}(t)}{\phi_i(s)}
 \mathbf 1_{\{t\ge m_i(s)\}}\,dt.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:derivative-weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:derivative-weights">8</a> proves that this is a probability kernel. The next exponent, gap, and ratio are $$x=\frac r{t+1},\qquad r'=\frac{rt}{t+1},\qquad s'=t.$$ The lower support endpoint ensures $x\le b$, and the upper-to-lower transition also satisfies $s'\ge2$. Put $g(t)=\log(1+1/t)$, so $r'=r e^{-g(t)}$.

<a id="eq:path-weight"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-arrival-cutoff"></a>
The harmonic transition $dx/x$, after reversing orientation in $t=r/x-1$, is $dt/(t+1)$. Dividing this by <a href="#eq:tilted-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tilted-kernel">[eq:tilted-kernel]</a> gives $$\frac{t^2}{(t+1)^2}\frac{\phi_i(s)}{\phi_{i'}(t)}
 =\left(\frac{r'}r\right)^2
    \frac{\phi_i(s)}{\phi_{i'}(t)}.$$ Consequently, for every finite prefix, $$\label{eq:path-weight}
 \text{original harmonic weight}
 =
 \text{probability weight}\,
 \left(\frac{r_{\rm end}}{r_{\rm in}}\right)^2
 \frac{\phi_{i_{\rm in}}(s_{\rm in})}
      {\phi_{i_{\rm end}}(s_{\rm end})}.$$ For a nonempty prefix its last exponent is $x=r_{\rm end}/s_{\rm end}$. Since successive cutoffs are nonincreasing, restriction of all exponents to $x>\ell$ is equivalent to the single arrival condition $$\label{eq:functions-arrival-cutoff}
 s_{\rm end}<r_{\rm end}/\ell.$$

<a id="eq:functions-start-range"></a>
In the rest of this section, starts are of even type with $$\label{eq:functions-start-range}
 s_{\rm in}\in[1.99,2.3].$$ Let $(i_n,s_n)$ be the resulting infinite sequence, $n=0,1,\ldots$, and put $$T_0=0,\qquad T_n=\sum_{j=1}^n g(s_j),\qquad
 r_n=r_{\rm in}e^{-T_n},\qquad x_n=r_n/s_n\quad(n\ge1).$$ After two steps the state spaces are $\{\mathrm e\}\times[2,\infty)$ and $\{\mathrm o\}\times[1,\infty)$. Only the first odd state can lie below $1$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Regeneration and invariant occupation

Call an odd state with $s\le3$ a regenerating state; regeneration is viewed as occurring just before its next draw. Its outgoing law is independent of $s$, since $\phi_\mathrm o(s)=A$ and $m_\mathrm o(s)=2$.

<a id="stmt-lem-regeneration"></a>
<div id="lem:regeneration" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Regeneration). *The time to first regeneration has an exponential moment uniformly over <a href="#eq:functions-start-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-start-range">[eq:functions-start-range]</a>. The cycles strictly after one regeneration through the next inclusive are iid. Their lengths $\tau$ have a finite exponential moment. Their total log decrements $$G=\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}g(s_j)$$ are positive, nonarithmetic, and have a finite exponential moment for some positive parameter. In particular $0<\mathbb EG<\infty$. Each complete cycle satisfies $G\ge g(3)=\log(4/3)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-regeneration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-regeneration</summary>

*Proof.* For sufficiently large current $s$, the excess of the next state over $s-1$ has tail $$\mathbb P(s_{\rm next}\ge s-1+h\mid i,s)
 =\frac{\phi_i(s+h)}{\phi_i(s)}\qquad(h\ge0).$$ Here the support minimum is $s-1$ for either type, and the identity follows from <a href="#eq:functions-derivative-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-derivative-integrals">[eq:functions-derivative-integrals]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:derivative-weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:derivative-weights">8</a> makes this tail at most $e^{-\lambda h}$, with any fixed large $\lambda$, outside a sufficiently large compact. Thus $V(i,s)=e^s$ satisfies $$\mathbb E(V(i_{\rm next},s_{\rm next})\mid i,s)
 \le \kappa V(i,s)+C,\qquad \kappa<1,$$ and the constant term can be omitted outside a larger compact. On the compact, finiteness and uniform boundedness of the expectation follow from the superexponential tails of the weights.

Choose a compact set $C_0$ and $\alpha>1$ such that $\mathbb E(V_{\rm next}\mid i,s)\le V(i,s)/\alpha$ outside $C_0$. Stopping the nonnegative supermartingale $\alpha^{n\wedge\sigma}V(i_{n\wedge\sigma},s_{n\wedge\sigma})$, where $\sigma$ is the first entrance to $C_0$, gives $$\mathbb E_{i,s}\alpha^\sigma\le V(i,s)$$ for starts outside $C_0$, by Fatou’s lemma. In particular the entrance time is almost surely finite.

From $C_0$ there are an integer $m$ and a probability $p>0$ such that regeneration is reached within $m$ steps with probability at least $p$, uniformly in the starting state. To see this, whenever $s>3$ choose the next state in the interval of width $1/4$ immediately above its support minimum; this lowers the state by at least $3/4$. An even state with $s\le3$ then draws into the regenerating odd range on the same choice. All prescribed intervals lie in a fixed compact and have uniformly positive transition probabilities.

Run such an $m$-step attempt, and on failure wait for the next entrance to $C_0$ before retrying. Let $D$ be the duration of one failed attempt together with this return. The stopped estimate and the uniform bound on the $V$-moment after the $m$ attempted steps show that, for some $\alpha_1>1$, $$\sup_{(i,s)\in C_0}\mathbb E_{i,s}
       [\alpha_1^D\mathbf 1_{\{\text{failure}\}}]<\infty.$$ For $0<\theta<1$, Hölder’s inequality bounds the same expression with $\alpha_1^\theta$ by $$\left(\sup_{C_0}\mathbb E[
       \alpha_1^D\mathbf 1_{\{\text{failure}\}}]\right)^\theta
 (1-p)^{1-\theta}.$$ For sufficiently small $\theta>0$ this is less than $1$. Summing over successive failed attempts proves an exponential moment for the hitting time of regeneration from $C_0$, and then from <a href="#eq:functions-start-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-start-range">[eq:functions-start-range]</a>. The same reasoning applies to a return after a regenerating start: its outgoing common draw has a bounded $V$-moment.

The common outgoing law proves independence and identical distribution of the complete cycles. During a cycle all odd states are at least $1$, and all even states are at least $2$, so each $g(s_j)\le\log2$. Thus the exponential moment of $\tau$ supplies an exponential moment of $G$ for a sufficiently small parameter. The terminal odd state lies in $[1,3]$, so its last decrement alone is at least $g(3)>0$.

For nonarithmeticity, consider a two-step return whose even state $t$ lies in a fixed subinterval of $(2,3)$ and whose next odd state $u$ lies strictly between $t-1$ and $3$. There is a two-dimensional region of such pairs on which the joint density is positive. On that region $G=g(t)+g(u)$ varies continuously and has a nonzero derivative in $u$. Its distribution therefore has a nonzero absolutely continuous component, and cannot be supported on an arithmetic lattice. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-invariant"></a>
<div id="lem:invariant" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:functions-invariant-densities"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-cycle-occupation"></a>
<a id="eq:mg-bound"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Invariant densities and cycle occupation). *On the ordinary even and odd state spaces, respectively, the finite raw invariant densities are $$\label{eq:functions-invariant-densities}
 \pi_\mathrm e(s)=\phi_\mathrm e(s)\quad(s\ge2),\qquad
 \pi_\mathrm o(s)=(1-s^{-2})\phi_\mathrm o(s)\quad(s\ge1).$$ Put $$\beta=\int_1^3\pi_\mathrm o(s)\,ds=\frac{4A}{3}.$$ For every nonnegative measurable state function $Q$, $$\label{eq:functions-cycle-occupation}
 \mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}Q(i_j,s_j)
 =\frac1\beta\sum_i\int Q(i,s)\pi_i(s)\,ds.$$ In particular, if $$M_g=\sum_i\int\pi_i(s)g(s)\,ds,$$ then $\mathbb EG=M_g/\beta$, and $$\label{eq:mg-bound}
 0<M_g\le4e^\gamma.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-invariant"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-invariant</summary>

*Proof.* For a new even state $t\ge2$, cancellation of the old derivative weight in the incoming integral gives $$\int_1^{t+1}(1-s^{-2})\,ds=\frac{t^2}{t+1}.$$ Multiplication by $W_0(t)\phi_\mathrm e(t)$ yields $\pi_\mathrm e(t)$. For a new odd state $t\ge1$, the corresponding integral is $\int_2^{t+1}ds=t-1$, which yields $(1-t^{-2})\phi_\mathrm o(t)$. These identities prove invariance. Finiteness follows from <a href="#eq:functions-phi-dickman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-dickman">[eq:functions-phi-dickman]</a>. On $[1,3]$, $\phi_\mathrm o=A$, so $\beta=A[s+s^{-1}]_1^3=4A/3$.

For completeness, normalize the raw measure to a stationary probability measure $\mu=\pi/M$, where $M=\sum_i\int\pi_i$. Start the chain with law $\mu$. For a bounded measurable state function $Q$, partition $Q(i_n,s_n)$ according to the last regenerating time strictly before $n$. If it is $n-k$, $1\le k\le n$, the common outgoing law shows that this term has expectation $$\mu(\text{regenerating states})\,
 \mathbb E[Q(i_k,s_k)\mathbf 1_{\{\tau\ge k\}}],$$ where the expectation on the right starts just after a regeneration. The remaining event is that there was no regenerating time among $0,\ldots,n-1$; its probability tends to zero by Lemma <a href="#lem:regeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regeneration">9</a> and dominated convergence over $\mu$. Stationarity and then $n\to\infty$ give $$\mu(Q)=\frac{\beta}{M}
       \mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}Q(i_j,s_j).$$ This proves <a href="#eq:functions-cycle-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-cycle-occupation">[eq:functions-cycle-occupation]</a> for bounded nonnegative $Q$, and truncation proves it in general. The argument does not require an aperiodic discrete-time chain; the alternating types cause no difficulty.

Since $g(s)\le1/s$, <a href="#eq:functions-phi-deficits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-phi-deficits">[eq:functions-phi-deficits]</a> gives $$M_g\le
 \int_2^\infty a(s)\,ds+
 2\int_1^\infty c(s)\,ds+
 \int_0^\infty a(s)\,ds.$$ The future integrals at $s=2$ give $\int_1^\infty c=2$ and $\int_1^\infty a=A-2$. As $a=1$ on $[0,2]$, the three displayed contributions are $A-3,\ 4,\ A-1$. Their sum is $2A=4e^\gamma$. Strict positivity is immediate from the positive even density and $g>0$. Taking $Q(i,s)=g(s)$ in <a href="#eq:functions-cycle-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-cycle-occupation">[eq:functions-cycle-occupation]</a> identifies $\mathbb EG$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Renewal limits and uniform band estimates

We use tests in the log-gap coordinate $u=\log r$. An admissible compact test $F_i(u,s)$ is bounded and continuous on the extended state ranges, with support in a fixed compact rectangle in $(u,s)$. We also permit multiplication by indicators of fixed intervals in $u$ or $s$, and by $\mathbf 1_{\{s<e^u/\ell\}}$, where $\ell=1$ or $2$. At the lower state endpoints one may first make a continuous one-sided extension to the extended range and then apply the indicator. Finite linear combinations of these tests are permitted. In particular, a continuous bounded test supported in a compact positive $r$-window and restricted by $s<r/\ell$ is covered: the restriction itself bounds $s$.

<a id="stmt-prop-occupation"></a>
<div id="prop:occupation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:functions-occupation-limit"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Compact occupation limit). *For an admissible compact test and starts satisfying <a href="#eq:functions-start-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-start-range">[eq:functions-start-range]</a>, $$\label{eq:functions-occupation-limit}
 \lim_{v\to\infty}
 \mathbb E_{s_{\rm in}}\sum_{n\ge0}F_{i_n}(v-T_n,s_n)
 =
 \frac1{M_g}\sum_i\int_{\mathbb R}\int F_i(u,s)\pi_i(s)\,ds\,du.$$ The convergence is uniform in $s_{\rm in}\in[1.99,2.3]$. The state integrals on the right use the ordinary invariant ranges in <a href="#eq:functions-invariant-densities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-invariant-densities">[eq:functions-invariant-densities]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-occupation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-occupation</summary>

*Proof.* First start immediately before the outgoing draw of a regeneration, and exclude that starting state from the sum. Write a complete cycle as $(i_j,s_j)_{1\le j\le\tau}$, and set $S_j=\sum_{k=1}^j g(s_k)$ within the cycle. Its response to the test is <a id="eq:functions-cycle-test"></a>$$\label{eq:functions-cycle-test}
 H_0(v)=\mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}F_{i_j}(v-S_j,s_j).$$ If the log-gap support of the test is contained in $[a,b]$, then $H_0(v)=0$ for $v<a$, since $S_j\ge0$. Also $S_j\le j\log2$, so for $v>b$, $$|H_0(v)|\le
 \|F\|_\infty
 \mathbb E\!\left[\tau\,
 \mathbf 1_{\{\tau\ge(v-b)/\log2\}}\right].$$ The exponential moment in Lemma <a href="#lem:regeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regeneration">9</a> gives an exponentially decreasing upper bound as $v\to\infty$.

The function $H_0$ is continuous, including for the permitted indicator tests. To verify this, condition on the history before the $j$-th draw and on the event $j\le\tau$, which is determined before that draw. The next state $t=s_j$ has a density. Writing $D=e^{v-S_{j-1}}>0$, its gap is $$e^{v-S_j}=\frac{Dt}{t+1}.$$ Equality at a fixed positive gap edge therefore specifies at most one value of $t$; equality at a fixed state edge does likewise. For the arrival boundary, $$t=e^{v-S_j}/\ell
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t+1=D/\ell.$$ Thus each boundary has conditional probability zero. For any fixed number of cycle steps dominated convergence gives continuity. The remaining steps are uniformly bounded in expectation by $\|F\|_\infty\mathbb E[\tau\mathbf 1_{\{\tau>N\}}]$, which tends to zero. This proves continuity of $H_0$. Continuity, the vanishing left tail, and the exponential right envelope show that $H_0$ is directly Riemann integrable.

<a id="eq:functions-renewal-convolution"></a>
Let $U_G$ be the renewal measure of successive complete-cycle decrements, including the boundary at zero: $$U_G=\sum_{k\ge0}\mathcal L(G_1+\cdots+G_k).$$ The expectation of the complete future test from a regeneration is $$\label{eq:functions-renewal-convolution}
 R_F(v)=\int_{[0,\infty)}H_0(v-y)\,U_G(dy).$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:regeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regeneration">9</a>, $G$ is nonarithmetic and has finite positive mean. The key renewal theorem <a href="#eq:renewal-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:renewal-input">[eq:renewal-input]</a> therefore gives $$R_F(v)\longrightarrow \frac1{\mathbb EG}\int_\mathbb RH_0(u)\,du.$$ Fubini is justified by the bounded test, its compact log-gap support, and $\mathbb E\tau<\infty$. Translating the log-gap integral within each cycle and applying <a href="#eq:functions-cycle-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-cycle-occupation">[eq:functions-cycle-occupation]</a> gives $$\int_\mathbb RH_0(u)\,du
 =\mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}\int_\mathbb RF_{i_j}(u,s_j)\,du
 =\frac1\beta\sum_i\int\!\!\int F_i(u,s)\pi_i(s)\,ds\,du.$$ Since $\mathbb EG=M_g/\beta$, this is the limit in <a href="#eq:functions-occupation-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-occupation-limit">[eq:functions-occupation-limit]</a>.

<a id="eq:functions-renewal-band"></a>
We record a boundedness fact needed to restore the initial segment. Every complete cycle has decrement at least $g(3)>0$. Consequently any interval of fixed length contains a bounded number of cycle boundaries on each sample path, and $$\label{eq:functions-renewal-band}
 \sup_{y\in\mathbb R}U_G([y,y+h])\le
 1+\left\lfloor\frac{h}{g(3)}\right\rfloor
 \qquad(h>0).$$ The compact bound and the exponential envelope for $H_0$, summed over unit intervals, show from <a href="#eq:functions-renewal-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-renewal-convolution">[eq:functions-renewal-convolution]</a> that $\sup_v|R_F(v)|<\infty$.

Now let $\tau_0$ be the first regenerating time from an allowed even start, and $D_0=T_{\tau_0}$. All initial per-step decrements are bounded by $c_*=\log(1+1/0.98)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:regeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regeneration">9</a> gives a uniform exponential moment for $\tau_0$, hence a uniformly tight, indeed exponentially bounded, family of $D_0$’s. The portion of the test through time $\tau_0$ tends to zero uniformly as $v\to\infty$: a contribution requires $c_*\tau_0\ge v-b$, and its absolute size is at most $\|F\|_\infty(\tau_0+1)$. Conditional on the initial history, the future after that state has expectation $R_F(v-D_0)$. It is the same function $R_F$ for every history, because the outgoing regenerating law is common. For a fixed $M$, convergence of $R_F(y)$ as $y\to\infty$ is uniform over $y=v-d,\ 0\le d\le M$. Uniform tightness of $D_0$ and boundedness of $R_F$ then give the claimed uniform limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-band-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:band-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:functions-band-from-regeneration"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-inverse-phi-band"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Uniform band bounds). *For every fixed $h>0$ there is $C_h<\infty$ with the following properties. For any nonnegative measurable state function $Q$ and any $v\in\mathbb R$, a regenerating start satisfies $$\label{eq:functions-band-from-regeneration}
 \mathbb E\sum_{n\ge1}Q(i_n,s_n)
        \mathbf 1_{\{T_n\in[v,v+h]\}}
 \le C_h\sum_i\int Q(i,s)\pi_i(s)\,ds.$$ From an even start in <a href="#eq:functions-start-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-start-range">[eq:functions-start-range]</a>, the same bound holds for the sum over $n\ge2$, uniformly in the starting ratio. The density of the first odd state is at most $$C\,\mathbf 1_{\{s\ge0.98\}}\phi_\mathrm o(s).$$ In particular, uniformly in $r_{\rm in}>0$, $R>0$, and $\ell\in\{1,2\}$, $$\label{eq:functions-inverse-phi-band}
 \mathbb E\sum_{n\ge1}
 \frac{\mathbf 1_{\{R\le r_n\le eR,\ s_n<r_n/\ell\}}}
      {\phi_{i_n}(s_n)}
 \ll R+1.$$ Including the starting state in the last sum changes its bound by at most an absolute constant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-band-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-band-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* For a regenerating start, condition on the internal states and decrements of one fresh cycle. Its beginning is a renewal boundary independent of that cycle. Tonelli’s theorem gives $$\mathbb E\sum_{n\ge1}Q(i_n,s_n)\mathbf 1_{\{T_n\in[v,v+h]\}}
 =
 \mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}Q(i_j,s_j)
 U_G([v-S_j,v+h-S_j]).$$ Apply <a href="#eq:functions-renewal-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-renewal-band">[eq:functions-renewal-band]</a> and then <a href="#eq:functions-cycle-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-cycle-occupation">[eq:functions-cycle-occupation]</a>. This argument permits arbitrary nonnegative $Q$: it never assumes independence of a state’s value and its internal decrement.

<a id="eq:functions-three-step-density"></a>
<a id="eq:functions-initial-density-domination"></a>
We next compare the initial even start to regeneration. If its ratio is $s_0$, the density after two draws, on $t\ge2$, is <a id="eq:functions-two-step-density"></a>$$\label{eq:functions-two-step-density}
 p_{s_0,2}(t)=
 \frac{W_0(t)\phi_\mathrm e(t)}{\phi_\mathrm e(s_0)}
 \int_{s_0-1}^{t+1}W_0(u)\,du.$$ Starting at regeneration, the density after three draws is $$\label{eq:functions-three-step-density}
 p_{{\rm reg},3}(t)=
 \frac{W_0(t)\phi_\mathrm e(t)}A
 \int_1^{t+1}W_0(u)
       \int_2^{u+1}W_0(y)\,dy\,du
 \qquad(t\ge2).$$ These formulas follow by canceling the intermediate derivative weights in the successive kernels. For $u\ge2$, the inner integral in <a href="#eq:functions-three-step-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-three-step-density">[eq:functions-three-step-density]</a> is at least $\int_2^3W_0(y)\,dy>0$. Since $t+1\ge3$, it follows that $$\int_1^{t+1}W_0(u)\int_2^{u+1}W_0(y)\,dy\,du
 \gg \int_2^{t+1}W_0(u)\,du
 \gg \int_{s_0-1}^{t+1}W_0(u)\,du,$$ uniformly for $s_0\in[1.99,2.3]$. The last comparison uses the bounded integral over $[0.99,2]$ and the positive lower bound for $\int_2^{t+1}W_0$. The starting denominator $\phi_\mathrm e(s_0)$ also has a positive compact lower bound. Thus $$\label{eq:functions-initial-density-domination}
 p_{s_0,2}(t)\le C p_{{\rm reg},3}(t)\qquad(t\ge2).$$

To use this marginal comparison, the correlated elapsed decrement must be removed explicitly. Let $I=[v,v+h]$, $C_2=2c_*$, and $C_3=3\log2$. The initial two decrements sum to at most $C_2$, so an event in $I$ at time $n\ge2$ has, measured from state $2$, a future decrement in $$I_2=[v-C_2,v+h].$$ Condition on that state, discard the initial decrement by this enlargement, and apply <a href="#eq:functions-initial-density-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-initial-density-domination">[eq:functions-initial-density-domination]</a>. For a path started at regeneration, its first three decrements sum to at most $C_3$; a future decrement in $I_2$ measured from state $3$ therefore gives a total decrement in $$[v-C_2,v+h+C_3].$$ The resulting expectation is bounded by a constant times the regenerating occupation sum in this enlarged band, which is covered by <a href="#eq:functions-band-from-regeneration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-band-from-regeneration">[eq:functions-band-from-regeneration]</a>. This proves the bound from even starts, including their state at time $2$, without imposing independence on the initial state and elapsed decrement.

At step $1$, the density is $$\frac{W_0(s)\phi_\mathrm o(s)}{\phi_\mathrm e(s_0)}
 \mathbf 1_{\{s\ge s_0-1\}}.$$ Both $W_0(s)$ for $s\ge0.99$ and $1/\phi_\mathrm e(s_0)$ on the allowed starts are bounded. This proves the asserted first-step estimate.

The condition $R\le r_n\le eR$ is a band of length $1$ in $T_n$. For $n\ge2$, use the preceding result with $$Q(i,s)=\frac{\mathbf 1_{\{s<eR/\ell\}}}{\phi_i(s)}.$$ The ratios of invariant to derivative densities are $1$ for the even states and $1-s^{-2}\le1$ for the odd states. Their integrals up to $eR/\ell$ are $O(R+1)$. The first-step density gives the same bound by direct integration. Finally $\phi_\mathrm e(s_0)$ has a positive compact lower bound, so the starting state’s possible contribution is $O(1)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-functions-compact-weight"></a>
<div id="rem:functions-compact-weight" class="remark">

*Remark 13*. Two consequences explain the later weight scales. At an arrival with $x_n>\ell$ and $r_n\le K$, <a href="#eq:functions-arrival-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-arrival-cutoff">[eq:functions-arrival-cutoff]</a> confines the state to a fixed compact with a positive lower endpoint. Hence $r_n^2/\phi_{i_n}(s_n)$ is bounded. Moreover, each such transition decreases the gap by more than $\ell$; there are only $O_K(1)$ such compact arrivals on one path. Formula <a href="#eq:path-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-weight">[eq:path-weight]</a> therefore bounds the total original harmonic weight of compact prefixes by $O_K(r_{\rm in}^{-2})$. If the counted prefixes can occur only on an event $\mathcal A$ of the tilted path, the bound is $O_K(r_{\rm in}^{-2})\mathbb P(\mathcal A)$.

Retain the restriction $x_n>\ell$, equivalently $s_n<r_n/\ell$, in the following tail estimate. After multiplication by $r_{\rm in}^2/\phi_\mathrm e(s_{\rm in})$, a reward $e^{-c r}$ in original harmonic weight is $r^2e^{-c r}/\phi_i(s)$ in tilted weight. Summing <a href="#eq:functions-inverse-phi-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-inverse-phi-band">[eq:functions-inverse-phi-band]</a> over the bands $[R,eR]$, $R\ge K$, gives a convergent upper bound whose tail tends to zero as $K\to\infty$, uniformly in the allowed starts. These statements also cover a fixed bounded factor in the reward.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Visits in a prescribed even-state window

<a id="stmt-lem-marked-visits"></a>
<div id="lem:marked-visits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Marked visits). *Fix $b_0>0$ and $\varepsilon>0$. There is a constant $C_\varepsilon>10$, independent of $b_0$, such that, whenever $b_1\ge C_\varepsilon b_0$ is fixed, the following event has probability at least $1-\varepsilon-o(1)$ as $r_{\rm in}\to\infty$, uniformly in <a href="#eq:functions-start-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functions-start-range">[eq:functions-start-range]</a>. At a regenerating odd state with pre-draw gap in $[10b_0,b_1]$, the next draw is an even arrival satisfying $$s\in[2.08,2.14],\qquad x\in[2b_0,b_1/2].$$ The proof yields the smaller ratio interval $[2.09,2.13]$; thus the stated ratio window has positive slack. The arriving gap exceeds $6b_0$, so, if $6b_0>K$, such a visit precedes every subsequent arrival of gap at most $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-marked-visits"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-marked-visits</summary>

*Proof.* Mark a complete regeneration cycle if its first draw lies in $[2.09,2.13]$. The common outgoing kernel gives a fixed probability $$p=\frac1A\int_{2.09}^{2.13}W_0(t)\phi_\mathrm e(t)\,dt
 \in(0,1).$$ The complete cycle objects, consisting of their state sequences and their decrements, are iid. Consequently the spacings $W$ between successive marked cycle beginnings are iid: each consists of one cycle conditioned on being marked, followed by a geometric number of cycles conditioned on being unmarked. More explicitly, the number $N$ of intervening unmarked cycles has $\mathbb P(N=m)=(1-p)^m p$, $m\ge0$; conditional on these marks, the cycle objects are independent with their respective conditional laws. The next marked cycle is excluded from the preceding spacing and begins the next such block. This proves independence of the spacings, even though a mark depends on its own cycle. It also gives $$\mathbb EW=
 \mathbb E(G\mid\text{marked})
 +\frac{1-p}{p}\mathbb E(G\mid\text{unmarked})
 =\frac{\mathbb EG}{p}<\infty.$$ Furthermore $W\ge g(3)>0$.

From an allowed initial start, the delay $D$ to the first marked beginning consists of the initial segment to regeneration and a geometric number of unmarked complete cycles. The first segment has uniformly bounded mean by Lemma <a href="#lem:regeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regeneration">9</a>; the latter sum has finite mean by the preceding conditional-cycle description. Thus $D$ is uniformly tight over the allowed initial states. The spacings after that beginning are independent of the delay.

We give the required wide-interval bound using only $\mathbb EW<\infty$. Let $U_W$ be the renewal measure for the marked spacings, with an initial renewal at zero. The lower bound on $W$ gives a uniform bound $C$ for its mass in any interval of length one. For $q\ge0$, if there is no marked renewal in $[q,q+H]$, some renewal at or before $q$ has its next spacing overshoot $q+H$. Independence of a renewal time and its next spacing therefore gives $$\mathbb P(\text{no renewal in }[q,q+H])
 \le\int_{[0,q]}\mathbb P(W>q+H-t)\,U_W(dt)
 \le C\sum_{j\ge0}\mathbb P(W>H+j).$$ Changing one endpoint in the unit-interval partition only changes the harmless absolute constant or shifts $H$ by $1$. The last quantity tends to zero as $H\to\infty$, since $W$ has finite mean. It is uniform in $q$. For the delayed process, condition on $D\le v$ and apply the same bound with $q=v-D$. The additional error $\mathbb P(D>v)$ tends uniformly to zero as $v\to\infty$.

Apply this with $$v=\log(r_{\rm in}/b_1),\qquad
 H=\log\bigl(b_1/(10b_0)\bigr).$$ Choose $H$ sufficiently large in terms of $\varepsilon$, equivalently choose $b_1/b_0$ sufficiently large, and then let $r_{\rm in}\to\infty$. A marked beginning in this log-decrement interval has pre-draw gap $r\in[10b_0,b_1]$. For its marked draw $t\in[2.09,2.13]$, $$\frac{10b_0}{3.13}\le x=\frac r{t+1}
 \le\frac{b_1}{3.09}.$$ This lies inside $[2b_0,b_1/2]$, and the arriving ratio is $t$. The new gap satisfies $$r'=\frac{rt}{t+1}\ge
