Let $U_t$ instead have the ordinary geometric probabilities $\mathbb P(U_t=j)=(1-1/t)t^{-j}$ for $j\ge0$. For $j\ge1$, $$\mathbb P(V_t\ge j)=\frac{t^{1-j}}{t-1}
 \ge t^{-j}=\mathbb P(U_t\ge j).$$ Couple $U_t,V_t$ monotonically, independently over $t$. Their mean difference is $$\mathbb E(V_t-U_t)
 =\frac{t}{(t-1)^2}-\frac1{t-1}
 =\frac1{(t-1)^2}.$$ It follows that the excess log-product $Z_E=\sum_{t\in E}(V_t-U_t)\log t$ has uniformly bounded mean, since $\sum_t\log t/(t-1)^2<\infty$. Extend the independent ordinary variables to all primes $t<P$. Then $$\prod_{t\in E}t^{U_t}\le\prod_{t<P}t^{U_t}.$$ The latter product has mass $V_{\rm all}(P)/n$ at every integer $n$ composed of primes below $P$. Every $n\le P^{.94}$ has this property, so $$\mathbb P\left(\prod_{t<P}t^{U_t}\le P^{.94}\right)
 =V_{\rm all}(P)\sum_{n\le P^{.94}}\frac1n
 =e^{-\gamma}(.94+o(1)).$$ By Markov’s inequality, $\mathbb P(Z_E>.01\log P)=O(1/\log P)$, uniformly in $E$. Outside this exception the preceding event implies $\prod_{t\in E}t^{V_t}\le P^{.95}$. Subtracting the exceptional probability proves <a href="#eq:stops-euler-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-euler-lower">[eq:stops-euler-lower]</a>; no independence between these last two events is required. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stops-euler-probability-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stops-euler-probability</summary>

<a id="eq:stops-m-coordinate"></a>
*Proof of <a href="#lem:stops-aligned-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stops-aligned-average">36</a>.* Every prime in the search bin eventually exceeds both $D$ and $P$. The alignment of the prime factors of $q'$ implies $(D,q')=1$; hence $(D,pq')=1$ for every $p\in\mathcal P$. The hypothetical hit condition and integrality of $n$ are therefore equivalent to $$\label{eq:stops-m-coordinate}
 Dn-A=pq'm,
 \qquad
 1\le\frac{A+pq'm}{D}\le Y,
 \qquad pq'm\equiv-A\pmod D.$$ We count a subfamily of these integers in which $m$ has one prime factor exceeding $P$. Averaging over $p$ then imposes the remaining avoidance conditions.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
###### A common interval for the auxiliary integer.

<a id="eq:stops-common-interval"></a>
Partition $\mathcal P$ into $\lceil w^{2C_s+10}\rceil$ equal-length subbins in prime coordinates. In one such subbin let $R'$ be its lower endpoint, let $V$ be its length, and put $$J^\circ=\frac{Y}{R'q'},\qquad s^\circ=\log_P J^\circ.$$ Uniformly over these subbins, <a id="eq:stops-subbin-size"></a>$$\label{eq:stops-subbin-size}
 \frac{V}{R'}\asymp\xi w^{-2C_s-10},
 \qquad J_p=J^\circ(1+o(1)).$$ Let $I$ be the intersection, over the real values of $p$ in the subbin, of the real $m$-intervals specified by the middle condition in <a href="#eq:stops-m-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-m-coordinate">[eq:stops-m-coordinate]</a>. The endpoints of one such interval are $$\frac{D-A}{pq'},\qquad \frac{DY-A}{pq'}.$$ Relative to $DJ^\circ$, the variation of either endpoint is at most $$O\left(\xi w^{-2C_s-10}
       \left(1+\frac{|A|}{DY}\right)\right)=o(1),$$ by <a href="#eq:stops-eligible" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-eligible">[eq:stops-eligible]</a>. Consequently $$\label{eq:stops-common-interval}
 |I|=(1-o(1))DJ^\circ.$$ The height of this interval satisfies $$\sup_{m\in I}|m|
 \le (D+|A|/Y)J^\circ(1+o(1))
 \le P^{s^\circ+.02},$$ provided $b_0$ is sufficiently large in terms of $C_s$. Indeed the extra factor is bounded by a constant times $w^{C_s+2}$, which is absorbed by $P^{.02}$ after such a choice. All these statements are uniform in the aligned progression family, its eligible rational, and the subbin.

<a id="eq:stops-factorization"></a>
Among primes $t<P$, distinguish those aligning to $A/D$ from those not aligning. Let $E$ be the set of nonaligned primes $t<P$ that do not divide $D$. We count only integers of the form $$\label{eq:stops-factorization}
 m=\pm d_0\ell',\qquad
 d_0\le P^{.95},\qquad
 t\mid d_0\Longrightarrow t\in E,\qquad
 \ell'>P\ \hbox{prime},$$ with $m\in I$. Such a representation is unique: its factor $d_0$ is less than $P$, while the distinguished prime factor is greater than $P$. Furthermore, $(m,D)=1$, since $d_0$ has no prime factor dividing $D$, and $D<P<\ell'$. If $t<P$ aligns to $A/D$, then $t$ divides neither $d_0$ nor $\ell'$, so $t\nmid m$, exactly as required to avoid the class at this aligned prime.

<a id="eq:stops-prime-factor-count"></a>
For each permitted $d_0$, the number of integers <a href="#eq:stops-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-factorization">[eq:stops-factorization]</a> in $I$, counting both signs, is at least $$\label{eq:stops-prime-factor-count}
 (1-o(1))\frac{|I|}{d_0(s^\circ+.02)\log P}.$$ Here the error tends to zero uniformly in $d_0$, $E$, and all the other data. To prove this, split $I$ into its positive and negative absolute-value pieces. Remove $|m|<P^{-.02}|I|$, and discard any remaining sign piece shorter than $P^{-.02}|I|$. The total removed length is $O(P^{-.02}|I|)$. After division by $d_0$, every retained interval has lower endpoint at least $$\frac{P^{-.02}|I|}{d_0}
 \ge(1-o(1))D P^{s^\circ-.97}>P^{1.04},$$ using <a href="#eq:stops-length-exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-length-exponents">[eq:stops-length-exponents]</a> and <a href="#eq:stops-common-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-common-interval">[eq:stops-common-interval]</a>. Its length divided by its upper endpoint is at least $(1-o(1))DP^{-.04}$, and hence at least $P^{-.05}$. If its upper endpoint is $v$, then $v>P^{1.04}$ implies $$vP^{-.05}\ge v^{9/10}.$$ The uniform short-interval prime number theorem in <a href="#lem:prime-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-inputs">4</a> therefore applies. Every upper endpoint has logarithm at most $(s^\circ+.02)\log P$. Summing the resulting lower bounds for the retained sign pieces proves <a href="#eq:stops-prime-factor-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-prime-factor-count">[eq:stops-prime-factor-count]</a>. In particular their prime factors automatically exceed $P$.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
###### Sieving the varying prime.

<a id="eq:stops-lift-fraction"></a>
Fix one of the integers $m$ just counted. The last congruence in <a href="#eq:stops-m-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-m-coordinate">[eq:stops-m-coordinate]</a> prescribes a single unit class for $p$ modulo $D$. This uses $(A,D)=(q'm,D)=1$. For each prime $t\mid D$, increasing $p$ by $D$ changes the integer $n$ by $q'm$, which is a unit modulo $t$. Thus avoiding $a_t$ removes exactly one of the $t$ lifts of this base class modulo $Dt$. Taken together, the remaining classes modulo $$Q=D\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(D)$$ form a set $\mathcal A$ of $\varphi(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(D))$ unit classes. Since $\varphi(Q)=\varphi(D)\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(D)$, their proportion among all unit classes modulo $Q$ is exactly $$\label{eq:stops-lift-fraction}
 \frac{|\mathcal A|}{\varphi(Q)}=\frac1D.$$ For $D=1$ this means the single unrestricted class and the same formula holds.

<a id="eq:stops-tag-prime-count"></a>
For $t\in E$, avoiding the original class is equivalent to $$pq'm\not\equiv Da_t-A\pmod t,$$ with nonzero right side. If $t\mid d_0$, this is automatic. If $t\nmid d_0$, then $m$ is a unit modulo $t$ because $\ell'>P$, and exactly one nonzero $p$-class modulo $t$ is forbidden. Thus the product of the remaining survival factors is $W_E(d_0)$ from <a href="#eq:stops-euler-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-euler-weight">[eq:stops-euler-weight]</a>. Let $N_{\mathcal I}$ be the number of primes in the present subbin $\mathcal I$. We claim that for any fixed $\gamma_1>0$, for all sufficiently large $z$, $$\label{eq:stops-tag-prime-count}
 \#\{p\in\mathcal I:\ p\ \hbox{gives a local survivor for this }m\}
 \ge(1-\gamma_1)\frac{N_{\mathcal I}}D W_E(d_0),$$ uniformly in all the data. If the product vanishes, the claim is trivial. In every other case, the restricted primes are at least $3$; their sieve densities $g(t)=1/(t-1)$ satisfy the hypotheses of <a href="#lem:fundamental" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fundamental">3</a>.

Here are the required progression and remainder bounds. Choose a fixed $A_2$ large enough, in terms of $\gamma_1$, to make the relative fundamental-lemma error small, and use sieve level $S=P^{A_2}$. For a squarefree intersection modulus $l\le S$ on the restricted primes and one class in $\mathcal A$, the forbidden conditions specify a single reduced class modulo $Ql$, since $(l,Q)=1$. Siegel–Walfisz from <a href="#lem:prime-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-inputs">4</a> gives its count as $$\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{li}}(R'+V)-\mathop{\mathrm{li}}(R')}{\varphi(Q)\varphi(l)}
 +O_{M_2}\left(\frac{R'}{(\log R')^{M_2}}\right).$$ The exponent $M_2$ can be any sufficiently large fixed constant. To check the modulus range, observe that <a id="eq:stops-sw-size-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:stops-sw-size-bounds}
 Q\le w^{2C_s},\qquad |\mathcal A|\le w^{C_s},\qquad
 S\le w^{b_1A_2},\qquad \log R'\ge w^{1/4}\log w.$$ Thus $Ql$, the level, and the lift count are each bounded by fixed powers of $\log R'$. The main term, for each base class, has the required multiplicative intersection density $1/\varphi(l)=\prod_{t\mid l}1/(t-1)$.

For completeness, let $C$ be the fixed divisor-weight exponent in the remainder of <a href="#lem:fundamental" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fundamental">3</a>. Summing over lifts and using the crude bound $\sum_{l\le S}\tau(l)^C\ll S^{C+1}$, the total remainder is at most $$O_{M_2}\left(
 |\mathcal A|S^{C+1}\frac{R'}{(\log R')^{M_2}}
 \right).$$ In a nonzero case, uniformly over subsets of restricted primes, $$W_E(d_0)\gg\frac1{\log P}.$$ Indeed the product over all $3\le t<P$ is of this order, by Mertens’ theorem and the convergent positive product in the identity $$1-\frac1{t-1}
 =\left(1-\frac1t\right)
   \left(1-\frac1{(t-1)^2}\right).$$ Also $N_{\mathcal I}\asymp V/\log R'$, uniformly by the same prime number theorem, or by Siegel–Walfisz with modulus one. Write $\kappa=2C_s+10$. The ratio of the displayed remainder to $(N_{\mathcal I}/D)W_E(d_0)$ is consequently at most $$O_{M_2,\xi}\left(
 \frac{D|\mathcal A|w^\kappa S^{C+1}\log P}
      {(\log R')^{M_2-1}}
 \right)
 \ll_{M_2,\xi}
 \frac{w^{4C_s+10+(C+1)b_1A_2}\log P}
      {(\log R')^{M_2-1}}=o(1)$$ on taking $M_2$ sufficiently large in terms of the fixed constants. This also justifies subtracting the two endpoint estimates for the short subbin. The logarithmic-integral main term can be replaced by $N_{\mathcal I}$ with relative error $o(1)$. Finally use <a href="#eq:stops-lift-fraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-lift-fraction">[eq:stops-lift-fraction]</a>, and take the fundamental-lemma relative error sufficiently small compared with $\gamma_1$. This proves <a href="#eq:stops-tag-prime-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-tag-prime-count">[eq:stops-tag-prime-count]</a>. Its error bound is uniform in $m$; large values of $m$ change only reduced residue classes.

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
###### Summing the lower bound and bounding an individual count.

For each $d_0$, first count the integers $m$ using <a href="#eq:stops-prime-factor-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-prime-factor-count">[eq:stops-prime-factor-count]</a>, and for each such $m$ count its admissible varying primes using <a href="#eq:stops-tag-prime-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-tag-prime-count">[eq:stops-tag-prime-count]</a>. A pair $(p,m)$ determines a unique integer $n$ by <a href="#eq:stops-m-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-m-coordinate">[eq:stops-m-coordinate]</a>. Unique factorization in <a href="#eq:stops-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-factorization">[eq:stops-factorization]</a> prevents duplicate counting for fixed $p$. Hence <a href="#lem:stops-euler-probability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stops-euler-probability">37</a> and <a href="#eq:stops-common-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-common-interval">[eq:stops-common-interval]</a> give, in one subbin, $$\sum_{p\in\mathcal I}T_p
 \ge (1-\gamma_1-o(1))
       \frac{N_{\mathcal I}J^\circ}{(s^\circ+.02)\log P}
       e^{-\gamma}(.94-o(1)).$$ Mertens’ theorem gives $V_{\rm all}(P)\sim e^{-\gamma}/\log P$. We have $s^\circ+.02\le2.22$ eventually, and $$\frac{.94}{2.22}>.423.$$ Choose $\gamma_1$ as a sufficiently small absolute constant; for example $\gamma_1=.05$ leaves a limiting coefficient greater than $.402$. Since $J_p=J^\circ(1+o(1))$ uniformly within a subbin, summing over all subbins proves <a href="#eq:stops-all-primes-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-all-primes-lower">[eq:stops-all-primes-lower]</a>. All relative errors used here are uniform, so the growing number of subbins and the number of pairs $(d_0,m)$ do not accumulate additional errors.

For the upper bound, the hit conditions give one ordinary progression of step $pq'$ and coordinate length $J_p+O(1)$. Every sieve prime below $P$ is coprime to this step. Apply the upper form of <a href="#lem:fundamental" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fundamental">3</a> using primes up to $P^\theta$, for a sufficiently small fixed absolute $\theta>0$, and a level such as $P^{1/2}$. The relative sieve parameter is a sufficiently large absolute constant; the summed $O(1)$ intersection remainders are $P^{1/2+o(1)}$, whereas $J_p\ge P^2$. Mertens’ theorem gives $$\prod_{t<P^\theta}(1-1/t)\ll_\theta V_{\rm all}(P).$$ This proves <a href="#eq:stops-individual-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-individual-upper">[eq:stops-individual-upper]</a> with an absolute $C_U$, independent of $C_s,b_0,b_1$ and of sufficiently small $\xi$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Comparison at the actual stops

The preceding lemma averages over every prime in a bin. To apply it to actual stops, we must show that fixing the other prime factors leaves the entire owner-aligning subset available. This is the role of the representative tests in the stop rule.

<a id="stmt-prop-stopped-comparison"></a>
<div id="prop:stopped-comparison" class="proposition">

**Proposition 38** (Comparison at stops). *There is an absolute constant $\eta_0>0$ with the following property. Fix $C_s$ and the regular-box parameters. Take $0<\eta\le\eta_0$, then take $b_0$ sufficiently large in terms of $C_s$, and take any fixed $b_1>b_0$. For all sufficiently small fixed $\xi>0$ and all sufficiently large $z$, the stops of <a href="#def:stops-rule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:stops-rule">35</a> satisfy <a id="eq:stops-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:stops-comparison}
 \sum_{d\ \mathrm{stopped}} S_d(x(P_d))
 \ge
 \sum_{d\ \mathrm{stopped}}\mu_d P_\mathrm e(r_d,x(P_d)),$$ where $P_d$ denotes the last prime of the tuple and both cutoffs are strict below $P_d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-stopped-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-stopped-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* We first explain exactly which prime is averaged. Partition the stopped nodes by their ordered bin labels, their tag position, and all prime choices other than the tag prime $p$. In a nonempty group, let $A/D$ be the owner of its tag bin. Then $p$ ranges over the entire subset of that bin aligning to $A/D$.

To justify this assertion, replace $p$ by any other aligning prime in the bin. All earlier choices remain fixed, so no earlier tag appears. At the designated position the new prime aligns to the same owner; hence the tag position and tag rational remain unchanged. Every subsequent all-prefix alignment test has the same truth value, since the other primes are fixed and the replacement aligns. The representative tests, lengths, and bin multiplicities depend only on the labels. Admission is preserved by <a href="#eq:stops-safety" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-safety">[eq:stops-safety]</a>, and the tag-bin multiplicity one prevents a change in prime ordering or a collision with another factor. These observations apply to each earlier prefix as well, so no earlier stop is introduced. This proves the assertion about the entire aligning subset.

In this group put $q'=d/p$. The last prime $P$ is a factor of $q'$ and is fixed. All primes of $q'$ are at least $P$, and they align to $A/D$. First allow *all* primes $p$ in the tag bin, assigning to $p$ the hypothetical hit class aligned to $A/D$. These hypothetical choices need not be actual stops; their local counts are auxiliary. Let $\mathcal P$ denote this tag bin, and use the hypothetical counts $T_p$ of <a href="#lem:stops-aligned-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stops-aligned-average">36</a>. On its aligning subset, $T_p=S_{pq'}(x(P))$.

For all choices of $p$ in the tag bin, put $b=x(P)$ and use the gap $r_{pq'}=\log_w(Y/(pq'))-a_\star+2$. Thus $$\log_PJ_p=\frac{r_{pq'}}b+\frac{a_\star-2}{b}.$$ The bin labels and representative safety tests are fixed, so the movement bound applies also to these hypothetical choices. By <a href="#eq:stops-actual-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-actual-window">[eq:stops-actual-window]</a>, after enlarging $b_0$ if necessary, all of them satisfy <a href="#eq:stops-length-exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-length-exponents">[eq:stops-length-exponents]</a>. The search bin is disjoint from the prime factors of $q'$ because the tag bin has multiplicity one. All hypotheses of <a href="#lem:stops-aligned-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stops-aligned-average">36</a> therefore hold.

<a id="eq:stops-reference-upper"></a>
For sufficiently large $z$, the actual cutoff lies in the fixed interval $[b_0-1,b_1]$. Applying <a href="#lem:local-reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-reference">24</a> on this interval, together with the product identity $V_{\rm all}(P)=V_0V(b)$, gives $$\frac{\mu_{pq'}P_\mathrm e(r_{pq'},b)}{J_pV_{\rm all}(P)}
 \le f(r_{pq'}/b)+O(1/b)+o(1).$$ The constants are uniform for the fixed enlarged window $[b_0-1,b_1]$ and for the ratio window in <a href="#eq:stops-actual-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-actual-window">[eq:stops-actual-window]</a>. On that ratio window, the near-$2$ formula and monotonicity of $f$ give $$f(r_{pq'}/b)\le f(2.18)
 <\frac{2(1.95)(.166)}{2.18}<.30.$$ Here $e^\gamma<1.95$ follows, for example, from $\gamma\le\sum_{j=1}^6j^{-1}-\log6<.66$, and $\log(1.18)<.166$ follows from $\log(1+u)\le u-u^2/2+u^3/3$ at $u=.18$. Thus first choosing $b_0$ large and subsequently taking $z$ large gives $$\label{eq:stops-reference-upper}
 \mu_{pq'}P_\mathrm e(r_{pq'},b)\le .33 J_pV_{\rm all}(P)$$ uniformly over the group and over its hypothetical choices.

Apply <a href="#eq:stops-all-primes-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-all-primes-lower">[eq:stops-all-primes-lower]</a> to the full tag bin. Let $\mathcal G$ be the aligning subset in the tag bin. Its complement contains at most an $\eta$ fraction of the bin primes. Since the $J_p$’s vary by at most $1+\xi$ within the whole bin, for $\xi\le1$ we have $$\sum_{p\notin\mathcal G}T_p
 \le 2C_U\eta V_{\rm all}(P)
                    \sum_{p\ \mathrm{in\ bin}}J_p.$$ Fix an absolute $\eta_0<1/2$ so small that $2C_U\eta_0<.05$. For $0<\eta\le\eta_0$, subtracting this bound from <a href="#eq:stops-all-primes-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-all-primes-lower">[eq:stops-all-primes-lower]</a> and using <a href="#eq:stops-reference-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-reference-upper">[eq:stops-reference-upper]</a> gives $$\begin{split}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal G}S_{pq'}(x(P))
 &=\sum_{p\in\mathcal G}T_p\\
 &\ge .34V_{\rm all}(P)\sum_{p\ \mathrm{in\ bin}}J_p\\
 &\ge\sum_{p\in\mathcal G}\mu_{pq'}P_\mathrm e(r_{pq'},b).
 \end{split}$$ The last inequality uses $.34>.33$ and positivity of $J_p$; it does not require a distribution hypothesis for the survivors inside the owner subset. Every stopped node belongs to exactly one of the groups considered. Summing the group inequalities proves <a href="#prop:stopped-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stopped-comparison">38</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### Almost every hard endpoint lies below a stop

We next explain why the same stops capture all but an arbitrarily small scaled harmonic mass of the hard compact endpoints. In what follows, the harmonic mass of a family of tuples is $\sum_d1/d$.

<a id="stmt-prop-hard-stopping"></a>
<div id="prop:hard-stopping" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:stops-hard-mass"></a>
**Proposition 39** (Stopping hard endpoints). *Fix a compact gap bound $K$, the regular-box parameters, the bounded candidate lists from <a href="#prop:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:inverse">29</a>, and the exponent $C_s$ from <a href="#cor:hard-endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:hard-endpoints">33</a>. Fix $0<\eta\le\eta_0$ as in <a href="#prop:stopped-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stopped-comparison">38</a>. For every $\delta>0$, take $b_0$ sufficiently large in terms of $C_s$ and $K$, then take $b_1/b_0$ sufficiently large in terms of the desired error, and finally take $\xi$ sufficiently small. For these fixed choices, the regular included hard endpoints with $r_d\le K$ having no stopped prefix have harmonic mass at most $$\label{eq:stops-hard-mass}
 (\delta+o(1))B^{-2}.$$ The eventual $o(1)$ may depend on all the fixed choices. In particular, $b_1$ and $\xi$ are fixed before $z$ tends to infinity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-hard-stopping"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-hard-stopping</summary>

*Proof.* For a hard endpoint, <a href="#cor:hard-endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:hard-endpoints">33</a> gives a rational on one of its candidate lists that satisfies <a href="#eq:stops-eligible" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-eligible">[eq:stops-eligible]</a> and aligns to every path factor of exponent greater than $C_s$. We need two facts about this path: its tag must be this rational, and it must have an earlier even visit in the stop window. We establish the tag statement in the full-box product measure, and the visit statement in the unconditioned path process. Their exceptional masses will then be added.

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
###### Identifying the tag.

Except for $o(B^{-2})$ mass, the path acquires this same rational as its tag during its search positions, as we now show.

Fix one regular full candidate box and one eligible rational $A/D$ on its list. Each of its search bins has multiplicity one, and <a href="#prop:regular-boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regular-boxes">26</a> supplies at least $c_7\log w$ such bins. For clarity, the count follows because all nonsearch factors together decrease the gap by at most $$C_{\rm len}\log B\,(w^{1/4}+h)=o(w^{1/2}).$$ The search factors come first and must therefore decrease the gap from $r_0\asymp B\asymp w\log w$ to at most $w^{1/2}$. A standard transition decreases the gap by at most a bounded multiplicative factor, including at the initial extended start. At least a constant multiple of $\log w$ search factors is necessary.

At a hard endpoint for the chosen rational, all these search primes align to it. If none of their bins is near-full for this rational, each of the bins has more than an $\eta$ fraction of nonaligning primes by count. For primes in $[R,(1+\xi)R]$, this is a harmonic fraction at least $\eta/(1+\xi)$. The search choices are independent in the full product box, so the fraction of its harmonic measure with all those alignments is at most $$\left(1-\frac{\eta}{1+\xi}\right)^{c_7\log w}=o(1).$$ This bounds the relevant hard event in the full box without asserting independence after conditioning on hardness.

Otherwise some visited search bin is near-full for this rational, which is therefore its owner. The hard endpoint’s choice in this bin aligns to its owner, so a tag has been acquired by that position. If the first tag is a different rational, the prime at the earlier tag position aligns both to $A/D$ and to the distinct eligible owner of that bin. By <a href="#eq:stops-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-overlap">[eq:stops-overlap]</a>, its possible choices form a uniformly negligible fraction, even after summation over $C_{\rm len}\log B$ positions. Indeed in a search bin this fraction is at most a constant, depending on fixed $\xi$, times $$\frac{L+\log R}{R},$$ and $R\ge\exp(w^{1/4}\log w)$.

There are at most the fixed list bound times the fixed candidate count such rational choices per box. The union of the preceding exceptions therefore costs $o(1)$ of the full candidate-box harmonic measure. The total mass of the enlarged boxes is $O_K(B^{-2})$, independently of sufficiently small $\xi$, by <a href="#prop:regular-boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regular-boxes">26</a>. Summing over the boundedly many candidates gives a total $o(B^{-2})$ exception. Outside it, a hard endpoint has the correct tag by the end of its search positions.

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
###### Finding an even visit before the compact endpoint.

<a id="eq:stops-marked-slack"></a>
Apply <a href="#lem:marked-visits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:marked-visits">14</a> to the continuous standard path. For any prescribed probability error $\varepsilon_1>0$, with $b_0$ fixed, choosing $b_1/b_0$ sufficiently large ensures, uniformly from the allowed starting ratios as the initial gap tends to infinity, an even arrival with ratio in $[2.08,2.14]$ and last exponent in $[2b_0,b_1/2]$, except with probability at most $\varepsilon_1+o(1)$. The proof of that lemma marks the first draw from an odd regeneration when its ratio lies in $[2.09,2.13]$, using a pre-draw gap in $[10b_0,b_1]$. In particular the arrival has the stronger interior margins $$\label{eq:stops-marked-slack}
 \frac{10b_0}{3.13}\le x\le\frac{b_1}{3.09},
 \qquad 2.09\le s\le2.13.$$ The choice of $b_1$ depends on the probability budget and fixed $b_0$, not on $z$ or on $\xi$.

Use <a href="#prop:prime-coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-coupling">17</a> for standard prime paths with lower exponent cutoff $1$. Actual included paths are a subset of these standard paths; their rules agree above exponent $2$. High-ratio histories ending in the fixed compact window have negligible scaled mass by <a href="#lem:high-states" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:high-states">16</a>. On the remaining histories the coupling matches states and exits, with a failure probability tending to zero. The interior margins in <a href="#eq:stops-marked-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-marked-slack">[eq:stops-marked-slack]</a> therefore give the asserted slightly wider visit windows on matched prime paths.

This visit lies in the segment being compared whenever the matched prime history ends in the compact window. In fact its arrival gap is at least a fixed multiple of $b_0$. Taking $b_0$ sufficiently large relative to $K$, that gap exceeds the matched compact endpoint gap. Since gaps decrease along the path, a raw continuous marked visit cannot occur after this endpoint. Matching exits puts the visit before any cutoff exit as well. Thus the coupling cannot lose the needed visit merely by stopping the compared segment at the compact endpoint.

The compact-history event estimate in <a href="#prop:prime-occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-occupation">19</a> now bounds the harmonic mass of compact endpoints missing such a visit by $$C_K(\varepsilon_1+o(1))B^{-2}+o(B^{-2}),$$ where $C_K$ depends on the fixed compact window and the starting bounds, not on the later bin width. Choose $\varepsilon_1$ sufficiently small in terms of $\delta/C_K$, and choose $b_1$ accordingly.

<a id="pm-h-54"></a>
###### Verifying the stop conditions.

Consider a regular hard endpoint with the correct tag and with this even visit. Every factor up to the visit has exponent at least the visit’s last exponent, which exceeds $C_s$ by the choice of $b_0$. Hence all these factors align to its tag rational. Its search positions have already passed: their lower exponents are at least $w^{1/4}$, whereas the visit exponent is bounded by fixed $b_1$. The tag is therefore attained by the visit, and its bin has multiplicity one. The visit-prefix length is at most the regular endpoint length $C_{\rm len}\log B$.

By regularity, every earlier nonempty even prefix, including the visit, has actual margin greater than $10\Delta$ in the admission test. The representative movement is at most $\Delta$, and changing the last exponent changes $\mathcal H$ by at most $2h$. For sufficiently large $z$, the representative margin is consequently at least $10\Delta-\Delta-2h>3\Delta$. Thus <a href="#eq:stops-safety" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-safety">[eq:stops-safety]</a> holds. Finally the ratio and exponent slack in the visit windows, the prefix movement bound, and a sufficiently small fixed $\xi$ imply <a href="#eq:stops-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-window">[eq:stops-window]</a>. All stop conditions hold at this visit. The branch has therefore stopped there or earlier.

Combining the $o(B^{-2})$ incorrect-tag exception with the arbitrarily small missing-visit mass proves <a href="#eq:stops-hard-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-hard-mass">[eq:stops-hard-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

The order of choices in these two propositions is worth making explicit. The constant $C_U$ in <a href="#eq:stops-individual-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stops-individual-upper">[eq:stops-individual-upper]</a> is absolute, so the owner threshold $\eta$ can be fixed before either stop-window endpoint. The choice of $b_0$ absorbs $C_s$, the local reference error, the interval-height margin, and the compact gap range. Only the ratio $b_1/b_0$ must then be enlarged to make the marked-visit error as small as desired. Afterwards $\xi$ is chosen for representative movements and all earlier freezing conditions. Every progression modulus and prime-sieve level used here remains a fixed power of $\log R'$ for these choices, however large the fixed $b_1$ is. The corresponding asymptotic thresholds may therefore wait until this final fixed $\xi$ has been chosen.

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Completion of the proof

We now combine the estimates, keeping the order of constants explicit. This first proves Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a>; the deduction of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> then uses only an upper sieve.

<a id="pm-h-56"></a>
#### The error budget

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-hard-stopping-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-hard-stopping</summary>

<a id="eq:assembly-reference"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a>.* By Proposition <a href="#prop:reference-margin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:reference-margin">22</a>, there is an absolute $c_L>0$ such that $$\label{eq:assembly-reference}
 P_\mathrm e(r_0,B)\ge\frac{c_L}{B^2}$$ for all sufficiently large $z$. Let $C_{\rm abs}$ be an absolute constant such that $$\left|N_d/\mu_d-1\right|\le C_{\rm abs}$$ at every included node; this follows from <a href="#eq:tree-small-counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-small-counts">[eq:tree-small-counts]</a>.

<a id="eq:assembly-tail"></a>
For nodes with large gap, the relative error in <a href="#eq:tree-small-counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-small-counts">[eq:tree-small-counts]</a> is $O(e^{-cr_d})$ after a fixed lower threshold on $r_d$. The tail estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-occupation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-occupation">19</a> permits us to choose a fixed $K$ so large that $$\label{eq:assembly-tail}
 \sum_{\substack{d\text{ included}\\r_d>K}}
 |N_d-\mu_d|
 \le\frac{c_L}{8}\frac{YV_0}{B^2}$$ eventually. Standard paths contain the included paths, so the nonnegative tail estimate applies. This choice depends only on the fixed reference margin and the absolute small-prime error constants.

<a id="eq:assembly-good-compact"></a>
The same proposition gives a constant $C_K$ for the total harmonic mass of nodes with $r_d\le K$. Choose $\varepsilon>0$ such that $\varepsilon C_K<c_L/8$. The compact nodes with relative error at most $\varepsilon$ then contribute at most $$\label{eq:assembly-good-compact}
 \sum_{\substack{d\text{ included}\\r_d\le K\\
                    |N_d/\mu_d-1|\le\varepsilon}}
 |N_d-\mu_d|
 \le\frac{c_L}{8}\frac{YV_0}{B^2}.$$

<a id="eq:assembly-beta"></a>
It remains to deal with compact nodes having relative error greater than $\varepsilon$. Fix a target $\beta>0$ so small that $$\label{eq:assembly-beta}
 C_{\rm abs}\beta<\frac{c_L}{8}.$$ We claim that the stopped tree can be chosen so that the retained bad compact nodes have total harmonic mass at most $\beta/B^2$. The following choices give this claim without requiring any constant to be chosen again at an earlier stage.

First use Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-witness">25</a> to fix a larger threshold $K_*$, a positive edge-discrepancy threshold, and a fixed maximum number $H_e$ of candidate positions. Choose the adjustable regularity exceptions in Proposition <a href="#prop:regular-boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regular-boxes">26</a> small enough to cost less than a prescribed small fraction of $\beta/B^2$. This fixes the path-length bound and the isolated-prime interval. For the moment leave the exception for near-threshold even prefixes to the eventual choice of bin width.

The enlarged regular boxes have total harmonic mass at most $C'_K/B^2$, with $C'_K$ independent of sufficiently small fixed $\xi$. Choose the exception fraction $\sigma$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:inverse">29</a> sufficiently small that $$H_eC'_K\sigma$$ is less than another prescribed small fraction of $\beta$. The inverse proposition now fixes a positive alignment fraction and a list bound $F$, independently of the eventual sufficiently small fixed width. Each list is attached to its entire candidate box.

Next choose the variance exception fraction in Proposition <a href="#prop:structured-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:structured-variance">32</a> small enough relative to $H_e$, $C'_K$, $F$ and $\beta$. A prime-count fraction in the isolated bin converts to a harmonic fraction at a bounded absolute cost. After this conversion and the finite unions over candidates and list elements, the total variance exception costs another prescribed small fraction of $\beta/B^2$. The variance proposition fixes a constant $C_s$. Corollary <a href="#cor:hard-endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:hard-endpoints">33</a> then shows that all remaining bad compact endpoints have a hard rational satisfying the global eligibility bounds and alignment of every factor with exponent greater than $C_s$.

Choose the absolute owner threshold $\eta$ required by Proposition <a href="#prop:stopped-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stopped-comparison">38</a>. Choose $b_0$ sufficiently large in terms of $C_s$ and $K$ and for the local reference and prime estimates in that proposition. Finally choose $b_1/b_0$ sufficiently large that Proposition <a href="#prop:hard-stopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hard-stopping">39</a> bounds the hard endpoints that remain outside stopped subtrees by the remaining allocated small fraction of $\beta/B^2$. This uses the marked-visit estimate with a prescribed failure probability, as well as the vanishing owner and wrong-tag exceptions for the fixed candidate lists.

All constants except the bin width have now been fixed. Choose a fixed $\xi>0$ sufficiently small for the interval-freezing errors, candidate-size slack, representative stopping margins and all earlier small-width requirements. In particular, the near-threshold exception, of cost $O(\Delta)+o(1)$ in the compact-history probability measure with $\Delta=2C_{\rm len}\xi$, can be made as small as the error budget requires. Then take $z$ sufficiently large for every preceding fixed choice. Asymptotic bounds whose thresholds depend on $\xi$ are used only at this final stage.

The sum of all these finitely many allocated costs is at most $\beta/B^2$, proving the claim. More explicitly, after removing the regularity exceptions, every retained bad endpoint has a witnessing edge. Outside the inverse exceptions it has an alignment in the fixed list. Outside the variance exceptions it is hard. Outside the hard-stopping exceptions it lies in a stopped subtree and is therefore not retained. This exhausts the cases. Fractional exceptions are summed using $H_eC'_K/B^2$, not the number of boxes.

The claim and <a href="#eq:assembly-beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-beta">[eq:assembly-beta]</a> show that retained bad compact nodes contribute at most $(c_L/8)YV_0/B^2$ in absolute error. Together with <a href="#eq:assembly-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-tail">[eq:assembly-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:assembly-good-compact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-good-compact">[eq:assembly-good-compact]</a>, this gives <a id="eq:assembly-retained"></a>$$\label{eq:assembly-retained}
 \sum_{d\in\mathcal T}|N_d-\mu_d|
 \le\frac{3c_L}{8}\frac{YV_0}{B^2}$$ for the retained expanded nodes of the chosen stopped tree. Proposition <a href="#prop:stopped-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stopped-comparison">38</a> gives $$\sum_{d\in\mathcal S}
 \bigl(S_d(b_d)-\mu_dP_\mathrm e(r_d,b_d)\bigr)\ge0.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:stopped-comparison-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stopped-comparison-identity">[eq:stopped-comparison-identity]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:tree-lower-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tree-lower-bound">6</a> and <a href="#eq:assembly-reference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-reference">[eq:assembly-reference]</a>. We obtain $$S_1(B)\ge\left(1-\frac38\right)c_L\frac{YV_0}{B^2}
 \ge\frac{c_L}{2}\frac{YV_0}{B^2}.$$ Every constant is independent of the prescribed residue classes. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-57"></a>
#### Removing the remaining divisor primes

The factor $\log w$ in the survivor estimate allows a cutoff smaller than $k\log k$. We choose it so that the loss from the at most $k$ remaining divisor primes is a fixed fraction of the surviving count.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-hard-stopping-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-hard-stopping</summary>

<a id="eq:assembly-survivor-lower"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* By Theorem <a href="#thm:survivors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:survivors">2</a> and Mertens’ formula, there is an absolute $c_0>0$ such that, for every sufficiently large real $z$, every assignment of one forbidden class at each prime $p\le z$ leaves at least $$\label{eq:assembly-survivor-lower}
 c_0\frac{Y\log w}{L^2}$$ integers in $[1,Y]$. Indeed, $V_0/B^2\sim e^{-\gamma}\log w/L^2$.

<a id="eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales"></a>
Let $k$ be sufficiently large, put $\ell=\log k$ and $M=\log\log k$, and take <a id="eq:assembly-jacobsthal-cutoff"></a>$$\label{eq:assembly-jacobsthal-cutoff}
 z=A_0\frac{k\log k}{\log\log k},$$ where the absolute constant $A_0>1$ will be fixed below, before $k$ tends to infinity. With the parameters of <a href="#eq:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameters">[eq:parameters]</a>, $$\label{eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales}
 L\sim\ell,\qquad \log w\sim M,\qquad
 Y\sim A_0^2\frac{k^2}{M^2},\qquad
 X:=\frac Yz\sim A_0\frac{k}{\ell M},\qquad
 \log X\sim\ell.$$ In particular $z\to\infty$ and $X\to\infty$ for every such fixed $A_0$.

Let $n$ be any positive integer with $\omega(n)\le k$, and let the given interval of $Y$ consecutive integers begin at an arbitrary integer $a$. Represent its integers as $a+j-1$, $1\le j\le Y$. For each prime $p\mid n$ with $p\le z$, prescribe the forbidden class $$j\equiv1-a\pmod p.$$ At the other primes $p\le z$, prescribe any class. The estimate <a href="#eq:assembly-survivor-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-survivor-lower">[eq:assembly-survivor-lower]</a> counts integers that avoid every divisor prime at most $z$, together with these additional restrictions.

<a id="eq:large-prime-deletion"></a>
Fix a prime divisor $q>z$ of $n$. Its divisibility class has at most $1+Y/q\le1+X$ representatives in the coordinate interval. Enclose them in a progression segment whose coordinate length is $\lceil X\rceil+2$. Fix a sufficiently small absolute $\theta>0$ and sieve this segment by primes at most $X^\theta$. This cutoff is below $z$ for large $k$, the step $q$ is invertible modulo every sieve prime, and the integers already counted in <a href="#eq:assembly-survivor-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-survivor-lower">[eq:assembly-survivor-lower]</a> avoid its prescribed class there. Lemma <a href="#lem:small-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:small-sieve">5</a> and Mertens’ formula therefore give $$\label{eq:large-prime-deletion}
 \#\{\text{these survivors divisible by }q\}
 \le C_1\left(1+\frac X{\log X}\right)$$ for an absolute $C_1$, uniform in $q$, $A_0$, the classes, and the translation once the displayed length conditions hold. More explicitly, the enclosing coordinate length is comparable to $X$ and at least $(X^\theta)^{1/(2\theta)}$ for large $X$, so the fixed exponent upper bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:small-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:small-sieve">5</a> applies. Enclosing a shorter or empty progression only increases the count; in particular the argument also covers $q>Y$.

<a id="eq:all-large-deletions"></a>
There are at most $k$ such further divisor primes. For each fixed $A_0$, <a href="#eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales">[eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales]</a> gives $$\label{eq:all-large-deletions}
 \frac{k(1+X/\log X)}{Y\log w/L^2}
 =\frac1{A_0}+o(1).$$ For the second term in the numerator, the quotient is $kL^2/(z\log X\log w)\to1/A_0$; the term $k$ is negligible since $Y\log w/L^2\asymp_{A_0}k^2/(\ell^2M)$. Choose once and for all $A_0>2C_1/c_0$. Equations <a href="#eq:large-prime-deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-prime-deletion">[eq:large-prime-deletion]</a> and <a href="#eq:all-large-deletions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-large-deletions">[eq:all-large-deletions]</a> then show that fewer than the number in <a href="#eq:assembly-survivor-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-survivor-lower">[eq:assembly-survivor-lower]</a> are removed, for all sufficiently large $k$. At least one integer in the original interval is therefore coprime to $n$.

This argument is uniform in the prime divisors of $n$ and in every integer $a$, including negative starting points and intervals containing zero. Prime multiplicities do not enter. The length estimate in <a href="#eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales">[eq:assembly-jacobsthal-scales]</a>, with $A_0$ now absolute, gives $$h(k)\ll\frac{k^2}{(\log\log k)^2}
       \ll\frac{k^2}{(\log\log(3k))^2}$$ for all sufficiently large $k$, since $\log\log(3k)/\log\log k\to1$.

To include the remaining finitely many values of $k$, suppose $n$ has $r\le k$ distinct prime divisors. Inclusion–exclusion gives at least $$m\prod_{p\mid n}\left(1-\frac1p\right)-2^r
 \ge\frac m{k+1}-2^k$$ coprime integers in every interval of $m$ consecutive integers. For the product bound, order the distinct primes as $p_1<\cdots<p_r$ and use $p_j\ge j+1$ to obtain $\prod_{j=1}^r(1-1/p_j)\ge1/(r+1)$. This also covers $r=0$ with the empty product equal to one. The integer $m_k=(k+1)2^k+1$ consequently bounds $h(k)$ for each remaining $k$. The maximum of $$\frac{m_k(\log\log(3k))^2}{k^2}$$ over this finite set, together with the constant already obtained for large $k$, supplies one absolute constant $C$ such that $h(k)\le Ck^2/(\log\log(3k))^2$ for every integer $k\ge1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hyperbola"></a>
### A modular hyperbola count with residue restrictions

The large-modulus case of Proposition <a href="#prop:structured-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:structured-variance">32</a> requires a rectangle count with a congruence restriction on the unit coordinate of a modular hyperbola. We give the completion argument with an explicit error, retaining multiplicities when an interval is longer than the modulus.

<a id="stmt-lem-hyperbola"></a>
<div id="lem:hyperbola" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hyperbola-explicit-count"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Completion with a unit residue restriction). *Let $H,T_0,T_1$ be positive integers such that $H\ge2$, $T_0$ is squarefree, every prime divisor of $T_0$ divides $H$, and $(T_1,HT_0)=1$. Put $N=HT_0$. Let $C_0\in\mathbb Z$ satisfy $(C_0,H)=1$, and prescribe the residue pattern <a id="eq:hyperbola-pattern"></a>$$\label{eq:hyperbola-pattern}
 p_0\in(\mathbb Z/T_0\mathbb Z)^\times,\qquad k_0\in\mathbb Z/T_0\mathbb Z,\qquad
 p_1\in(\mathbb Z/T_1\mathbb Z)^\times,\qquad m_1\in\mathbb Z/T_1\mathbb Z.$$ The residue classes modulo $1$ have their usual unique interpretation. Let $I,J$ be bounded real intervals of lengths $X,Y_2\ge0$, with either endpoint independently included or excluded. For a positive integer $l$ with $(l,HT_1)=1$, let $C_l$ be the number of integer pairs $(p,m)$ satisfying <a id="eq:hyperbola-counted-set"></a>$$\label{eq:hyperbola-counted-set}
\begin{gathered}
 p\in I,\qquad m\in J,\qquad l\mid p,\\
 p\equiv p_0\pmod{T_0},\qquad
 p\equiv p_1\pmod{T_1},\qquad
 m\equiv m_1\pmod{T_1},\\
 pm\equiv C_0+Hk_0\pmod N.
\end{gathered}$$ Here $p$ is an integer variable, without a primality condition. Uniformly in all these data, $$\label{eq:hyperbola-explicit-count}
 C_l=
 \frac{XY_2\varphi(N)}{lT_1^2N^2\varphi(T_0)}
 +O\left(
 \tau(N)^2\log^2(2N)\sqrt{NT_0}
 \left(1+\frac{X}{lN}+\frac{Y_2}{N}\right)
 \right),$$ with an absolute implied constant.*

<a id="eq:hyperbola-application-range"></a>
<a id="eq:hyperbola-application-count"></a>
*In particular, suppose that $R\to\infty$ and the data range uniformly over families satisfying $$\label{eq:hyperbola-application-range}
 R^{4/5}<H\le R\mathcal Z^{11},\qquad
 X\ll R,\qquad Y_2\le3H,\qquad
 \mathcal Z,T_0,T_1=R^{o(1)}.$$ Then, uniformly over all the patterns and intervals above and all squarefree $l\le R^{1/10}$ with $(l,HT_1)=1$, $$\label{eq:hyperbola-application-count}
 C_l=
 \frac{XY_2\varphi(N)}{lT_1^2N^2\varphi(T_0)}
 +O\bigl(R^{3/5+o(1)}\bigr).$$ Thus the same assertion holds with error $O(R^{7/10+o(1)})$. Uniformity in the last two assertions means that the upper bounds represented by $R^{o(1)}$ in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-application-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-application-range">[eq:hyperbola-application-range]</a> hold uniformly over the family; equivalently, for every $\delta>0$ the error in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-application-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-application-count">[eq:hyperbola-application-count]</a> is $O_\delta(R^{3/5+\delta})$ for sufficiently large $R$ in that family. No lower bound for $X/l$ is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hyperbola"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hyperbola</summary>

<a id="eq:hyperbola-weil"></a>
*Proof.* Write $e_n(t)=\exp(2\pi i t/n)$. We use the classical complete Kloosterman estimate, in the form recorded in (Lichtman 2022, Lemma 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Lichtman2022](#ref-Lichtman2022)]: $$\label{eq:hyperbola-weil}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Kl}}(a,b;n):=\sum_{\substack{u\bmod n\\(u,n)=1}}
                  e_n(au+b\bar u),
 \qquad
 |\mathop{\mathrm{Kl}}(a,b;n)|\ll\tau(n)n^{1/2}(a,b,n)^{1/2}.$$ This bound permits arbitrary composite $n$ and arbitrary integer frequencies, including zero frequencies. The inverse $\bar u$ is taken modulo $n$.

<a id="pm-h-59"></a>
###### Changing the first coordinate.

Choose a representative of $k_0$ and put $c=C_0+Hk_0$ modulo $N$. The integers $H$ and $N$ have the same prime support. Since $(C_0,H)=1$, it follows that $(c,N)=1$. The hypothesis on $l$ also implies $(l,NT_1)=1$. On setting $p=lv$, define $$\begin{gathered}
 U=X/l,\qquad V=Y_2,\qquad c'=c\bar l\pmod N,\\
 a_0=p_0\bar l\pmod{T_0},\qquad
 a_1=p_1\bar l\pmod{T_1}.
 \end{gathered}$$ Every inverse here is taken in its indicated modulus. The interval $I_l=\{x/l:x\in I\}$ has length $U$ and the induced endpoint conventions. The pairs now satisfy $$\begin{gathered}
 v\in I_l,\quad v\equiv a_1\pmod{T_1},\qquad
 m\in J,\quad m\equiv m_1\pmod{T_1},\\
 v\equiv a_0\pmod{T_0},\qquad vm\equiv c'\pmod N.
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular $v$ and $m$ are automatically units modulo $N$.

<a id="pm-h-60"></a>
###### Fourier weights and their divisor-weighted sums.

<a id="eq:hyperbola-fourier-weights"></a>
<a id="eq:hyperbola-zero-weights"></a>
<a id="eq:hyperbola-geometric"></a>
For $h,k\bmod N$, define the unnormalized weights $$\label{eq:hyperbola-fourier-weights}
 A(h)=\sum_{\substack{v\in I_l\cap\mathbb Z\\v\equiv a_1\ (T_1)}}e_N(-hv),
 \qquad
 B(k)=\sum_{\substack{m\in J\cap\mathbb Z\\m\equiv m_1\ (T_1)}}e_N(-km).$$ These are sums over the integers in the progressions, so a residue modulo $N$ is counted with its full multiplicity. They therefore apply unchanged when $U$ or $V$ exceeds $N$. Counting the progressions gives $$\label{eq:hyperbola-zero-weights}
 A(0)=U/T_1+O(1),\qquad B(0)=V/T_1+O(1).$$ For a nonzero residue $h\bmod N$, let $|h|_N=\min_{j\in\mathbb Z}|h+jN|$. A geometric-progression sum, with ratio $e_N(-hT_1)\ne1$, gives $$\label{eq:hyperbola-geometric}
 |A(h)|,\ |B(h)|\ll\frac{N}{|hT_1|_N}\qquad(h\not\equiv0\pmod N).$$ The right side is independent of the number of complete periods and of the locations of the intervals.

<a id="eq:hyperbola-fourier-sums"></a>
Multiplication by $T_1$ permutes the nonzero residues modulo $N$ and preserves their greatest common divisors with $N$. Thus <a id="eq:hyperbola-gcd-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:hyperbola-gcd-sum}
\begin{split}
 \sum_{h\ne0\ (N)}\frac{(h,N)^{1/2}}{|hT_1|_N}
 &=\sum_{r\ne0\ (N)}\frac{(r,N)^{1/2}}{|r|_N}\\
 &\le\sum_{d\mid N}d^{1/2}
       \sum_{\substack{r\ne0\ (N)\\d\mid r}}\frac1{|r|_N}\\
 &\ll\log(2N)\sum_{d\mid N}d^{-1/2}
 \le\tau(N)\log(2N).
\end{split}$$ Indeed the inner sum is at most $2d^{-1}\sum_{1\le j\le N/(2d)}j^{-1}$, with a harmless possible overcount of the midpoint. Without the gcd weight the corresponding sum is $O(\log(2N))$. In conjunction with <a href="#eq:hyperbola-geometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-geometric">[eq:hyperbola-geometric]</a>, this proves $$\label{eq:hyperbola-fourier-sums}
\begin{gathered}
 \sum_{h\ne0\ (N)}|A(h)|\ll N\log(2N),\qquad
 \sum_{h\ne0\ (N)}|A(h)|(h,N)^{1/2}
       \ll N\tau(N)\log(2N),
\end{gathered}$$ and the identical estimates with $B$ in place of $A$.

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
###### Completion with the unit restriction.

<a id="eq:hyperbola-completion-identity"></a>
Define $$S(h,k)=
 \sum_{\substack{u\bmod N\\(u,N)=1\\u\equiv a_0\ (T_0)}}
       e_N(hu+kc'\bar u).$$ Fourier inversion, with the multiplicities in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-fourier-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-fourier-weights">[eq:hyperbola-fourier-weights]</a>, gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:hyperbola-completion-identity}
 C_l=\frac1{N^2}\sum_{h,k\bmod N}A(h)B(k)S(h,k).$$ For example, summing $A(h)e_N(hu)/N$ over $h$ counts all allowed integers $v\equiv u\pmod N$, which verifies the normalization directly.

<a id="eq:hyperbola-second-nonzero"></a>
<a id="eq:hyperbola-shifted-gcd"></a>
<a id="eq:hyperbola-first-nonzero"></a>
Orthogonality modulo $T_0$ also gives the exact formula <a id="eq:hyperbola-restricted-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:hyperbola-restricted-sum}
 S(h,k)=\frac1{T_0}\sum_{j\bmod T_0}
       e_{T_0}(-ja_0)\mathop{\mathrm{Kl}}(h+jH,kc';N).$$ The average over $j$ does not cost a factor $T_0$ in the following absolute bounds. When $k\ne0\pmod N$, the fact that $(c',N)=1$ and <a href="#eq:hyperbola-weil" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-weil">[eq:hyperbola-weil]</a> give $$\label{eq:hyperbola-second-nonzero}
 |S(h,k)|\ll\tau(N)\sqrt N\,(k,N)^{1/2},
 \qquad k\ne0\pmod N,$$ uniformly also when $h=0$. For $h\ne0$ and $k=0$, observe that $$\label{eq:hyperbola-shifted-gcd}
 (h+jH,N)\le T_0(h,N).$$ In fact any common divisor of $h+jH$ and $N$ divides $T_0h$, because $T_0H=N$, and $(T_0h,N)\le T_0(h,N)$. Consequently $$\label{eq:hyperbola-first-nonzero}
 |S(h,0)|\ll\tau(N)\sqrt{NT_0}\,(h,N)^{1/2},
 \qquad h\ne0\pmod N.$$ If a shift $h+jH$ vanishes modulo $N$, it is still covered by <a href="#eq:hyperbola-shifted-gcd" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-shifted-gcd">[eq:hyperbola-shifted-gcd]</a>; no shifted zero frequency is omitted. No squarefreeness of $H$ or $N$ has been used.

At the two original zero frequencies, <a id="eq:hyperbola-complete-zero"></a>$$\label{eq:hyperbola-complete-zero}
 S(0,0)=\frac{\varphi(N)}{\varphi(T_0)},\qquad
 \varphi(N)=T_0\varphi(H).$$ For the first equality, reduction from the units modulo $N$ onto the units modulo $T_0$ is surjective with equal fibers. Explicitly, if $t^a$ exactly divides $H$ and $t\mid T_0$, then the local modulus in $N$ is $t^{a+1}$, and every unit modulo $t$ has $t^a$ unit lifts. At a prime of $H$ outside $T_0$ there is no prescribed local class. The Chinese remainder theorem proves the first equality. The same prime-power calculation gives the second.

<a id="pm-h-62"></a>
###### The four frequency cases.

<a id="eq:hyperbola-main-term"></a>
The contribution of $h=k=0$ to <a href="#eq:hyperbola-completion-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-completion-identity">[eq:hyperbola-completion-identity]</a> is $$\label{eq:hyperbola-main-term}
 \frac{A(0)B(0)\varphi(N)}{N^2\varphi(T_0)}
 =\frac{UV\varphi(N)}{T_1^2N^2\varphi(T_0)}
   +O\left(\frac{U+V+1}{N}\right),$$ where <a href="#eq:hyperbola-zero-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-zero-weights">[eq:hyperbola-zero-weights]</a> handles every endpoint convention. This is the stated main term since $UV=XY_2/l$.

For $h\ne0$, $k\ne0$, use <a href="#eq:hyperbola-second-nonzero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-second-nonzero">[eq:hyperbola-second-nonzero]</a> and <a href="#eq:hyperbola-fourier-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-fourier-sums">[eq:hyperbola-fourier-sums]</a>. The total absolute contribution is $$\ll\frac{\tau(N)\sqrt N}{N^2}
       \bigl(N\log(2N)\bigr)
       \bigl(N\tau(N)\log(2N)\bigr)
 =\tau(N)^2\sqrt N\log^2(2N).$$ For $h=0$, $k\ne0$, the same argument with the zero weight retained gives $$\ll A(0)\tau(N)^2N^{-1/2}\log(2N).$$ For $h\ne0$, $k=0$, instead use <a href="#eq:hyperbola-first-nonzero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-first-nonzero">[eq:hyperbola-first-nonzero]</a>; this contribution is $$\ll B(0)\tau(N)^2T_0^{1/2}N^{-1/2}\log(2N).$$ Finally, $A(0)\ll U/T_1+1$ and $B(0)\ll V/T_1+1$. These three estimates and the rounding term in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-main-term" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-main-term">[eq:hyperbola-main-term]</a> are all bounded by the error displayed in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-explicit-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-explicit-count">[eq:hyperbola-explicit-count]</a>. This proves the general assertion, with an absolute implied constant.

<a id="pm-h-63"></a>
###### The size range needed in the variance argument.

Under <a href="#eq:hyperbola-application-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-application-range">[eq:hyperbola-application-range]</a>, $$R^{4/5}<N\le R^{1+o(1)},\qquad Y_2\le3N.$$ The elementary divisor bound $\tau(N)=N^{o(1)}$ and $T_0=R^{o(1)}$ turn the explicit error into $$R^{o(1)}\left(\sqrt N+\frac{X/l}{\sqrt N}
                           +\frac{Y_2}{\sqrt N}\right)
 \ll R^{1/2+o(1)}+R^{3/5+o(1)}
 =R^{3/5+o(1)}.$$ The estimate uses only $l\ge1$, and is therefore uniform throughout the asserted sieve range. It also holds for shorter blocks and for arbitrarily translated intervals. This proves <a href="#eq:hyperbola-application-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-application-count">[eq:hyperbola-application-count]</a> and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-hyperbola-small-patterns"></a>
<div id="rem:hyperbola-small-patterns" class="remark">

*Remark 41*. In the application, $R\ge z^{\alpha_{\min}}$ with fixed $\alpha_{\min}>0$, $\mathcal Z=\exp(L/\log L)$, and $$T_0=\prod_{\substack{t\le w\\t\mid H}}t,\qquad
 T_1=\prod_{\substack{t\le w\\t\nmid H}}t.$$ Then $\log(T_0T_1)=O(w)=o(\log R)$ and $\log\mathcal Z=o(\log R)$, uniformly in the isolated bin. Thus the uniform family conditions in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-application-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-application-range">[eq:hyperbola-application-range]</a> follow from the parameters of the main argument. A fixed bin-width parameter may affect the point at which these asymptotics apply, but it does not enter the absolute constant in <a href="#eq:hyperbola-explicit-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbola-explicit-count">[eq:hyperbola-explicit-count]</a>.

</div>

<a id="sec:elementary-inputs"></a>
### Elementary forms of four auxiliary estimates

We give the versions of four classical estimates needed in the proof. The Buchstab normalization follows directly from its delay equation; the large-sieve and plane-curve bounds need only elementary integration and elimination. The progression upper bound is a consequence of Lemma <a href="#lem:fundamental" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fundamental">3</a> and Mertens’ product formula. In particular, the last argument retains the fundamental lemma as an input.

<a id="pm-h-65"></a>
#### The continuous Buchstab normalization

The normalization used in Lemma <a href="#lem:future-integrals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:future-integrals">7</a> is the classical one; see (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan, n.d.b, sec. 28.9, p. 236). The following proof uses only the continuous delay equation, independently of the positivity argument for the sieve functions.

<a id="stmt-lem-elementary-buchstab"></a>
<div id="lem:elementary-buchstab" class="lemma">

**Lemma 42**. *Let $\omega:[1,\infty)\to\mathbb R$ be the continuous function determined by $$u\omega(u)=1\quad(1\le u\le2),\qquad
 (u\omega(u))'=\omega(u-1)\quad(u>2).$$ Then $\omega(u)\to e^{-\gamma}$ as $u\to\infty$, where $\gamma=\lim_{n\to\infty}(H_n-\log n)$ and $H_n=\sum_{j=1}^n1/j$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-elementary-buchstab"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-elementary-buchstab</summary>

<a id="eq:elementary-buchstab-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* Successive integration on unit intervals constructs a unique positive continuous function, locally absolutely continuous and with bounded one-sided derivatives on each such interval. For $u>2$ its equation gives, almost everywhere, $$\label{eq:elementary-buchstab-derivative}
 u\omega'(u)=\omega(u-1)-\omega(u)
             =-\int_{u-1}^{u}\omega'(v)\,dv.$$ Put $M_n=\mathop{\rm ess\,sup}_{n<u<n+1}|\omega'(u)|$. These quantities are finite by the construction, and $M_1\le1$. For every integer $n\ge2$, <a href="#eq:elementary-buchstab-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:elementary-buchstab-derivative">[eq:elementary-buchstab-derivative]</a> implies $$M_n\le\frac1n\max(M_{n-1},M_n).$$ If $M_n>M_{n-1}$ this inequality forces $M_n=0$, a contradiction; otherwise it gives $M_n\le M_{n-1}/n$. Thus $M_n\le1/n!$. In particular $\omega'$ is integrable on $[1,\infty)$, so $\omega$ is bounded and has a finite limit, say $\ell$.

<a id="eq:elementary-buchstab-transform"></a>
<a id="eq:elementary-euler-integral"></a>
It remains to determine $\ell$. For $t>0$ define $$W(t)=\int_1^\infty\omega(u)e^{-tu}\,du.$$ Boundedness justifies differentiation and integration by parts. The derivative of $u\omega(u)$ is zero on $(1,2)$; integrating it on $[1,\infty)$, including the boundary value at $1$, gives $$-e^{-t}-tW'(t)
   =\int_2^\infty\omega(u-1)e^{-tu}\,du
   =e^{-t}W(t).$$ Since $W(t)\to0$ as $t\to\infty$, solving this differential equation yields $$\label{eq:elementary-buchstab-transform}
 1+W(t)=\exp\!\left(\int_t^\infty\frac{e^{-s}}s\,ds\right).$$ The constant in this formula can be evaluated without a Tauberian argument. Integration by parts shows that $$\int_t^\infty\frac{e^{-s}}s\,ds+\log t
  =(1-e^{-t})\log t+\int_t^\infty e^{-s}\log s\,ds.$$ Moreover, $$\label{eq:elementary-euler-integral}
 \int_0^\infty e^{-s}\log s\,ds=-\gamma.$$ For an elementary verification, substitution $s=nv$ and integration of the geometric sum give $$\int_0^n(1-s/n)^n\log s\,ds
   =\frac{n}{n+1}\bigl(\log n-H_{n+1}\bigr).$$ Indeed, $$\int_0^1(1-v)^n\log v\,dv
  =-\frac1{n+1}\int_0^1\frac{1-(1-v)^{n+1}}v\,dv
  =-\frac{H_{n+1}}{n+1}.$$ The integrands in the first identity, extended by zero for $s>n$, are bounded in absolute value by $e^{-s}|\log s|$. Dominated convergence proves <a href="#eq:elementary-euler-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:elementary-euler-integral">[eq:elementary-euler-integral]</a>. Consequently <a href="#eq:elementary-buchstab-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:elementary-buchstab-transform">[eq:elementary-buchstab-transform]</a> gives $tW(t)\to e^{-\gamma}$ as $t\downarrow0$. On the other hand, substituting $v=tu$ and using bounded convergence gives $$tW(t)=\int_t^\infty\omega(v/t)e^{-v}\,dv\longrightarrow\ell.$$ This identifies $\ell=e^{-\gamma}$. ◻

</details>

This derivation supplies precisely the independently normalized limit needed to rule out a jump at $2$ in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:future-integrals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:future-integrals">7</a>. It makes no use of the auxiliary Markov chain or of the future-integral identities proved there.

<a id="pm-h-66"></a>
#### The additive large sieve at the required order

The sharp large sieve is due to Montgomery and Vaughan (H. L. Montgomery and Vaughan 1973) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan1973](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan1973)]; see also (Robert C. Vaughan 2025) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[VaughanLargeSieve](#ref-VaughanLargeSieve)]. The following separated-arcs argument gives the order of <a href="#eq:large-sieve-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-sieve-input">[eq:large-sieve-input]</a> that is used in the inverse estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-elementary-large-sieve"></a>
<div id="lem:elementary-large-sieve" class="lemma">

**Lemma 43**. *Let $x_1,\ldots,x_R\in\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ have pairwise circular distance at least $\delta$, where $0<\delta\le1$. Let $J\ge1$ be an integer. If complex coefficients $b_j$ are supported on $J$ consecutive integers, then $$\sum_{r=1}^{R}\left|\sum_j b_j e(jx_r)\right|^2
   \ll (J+\delta^{-1})\sum_j|b_j|^2$$ with an absolute implied constant. Hence <a href="#eq:large-sieve-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-sieve-input">[eq:large-sieve-input]</a> holds for every real $Q\ge1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-elementary-large-sieve"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-elementary-large-sieve</summary>

*Proof.* Shifting the frequencies multiplies their sum by a complex number of modulus one. We may therefore write $T(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}b_j e(jx)$ and $f(x)=|T(x)|^2$. Let $I_r$ be the arc of length $\delta$ centred at $x_r$. Their interiors are disjoint. For $t\in I_r$, the fundamental theorem of calculus along that arc gives $$f(x_r)\le f(t)+\int_{I_r}|f'(x)|\,dx.$$ Average over $t\in I_r$ and sum over $r$ to obtain $$\sum_r f(x_r)
 \le\delta^{-1}\int_0^1|T(x)|^2\,dx
       +\int_0^1|f'(x)|\,dx.$$ Since $|f'|\le2|T'||T|$, Cauchy–Schwarz and Parseval give $$\int_0^1|f'(x)|\,dx
 \le2\|T'\|_2\|T\|_2
 \le4\pi(J-1)\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}|b_j|^2.$$ This proves the asserted estimate. Distinct reduced fractions modulo one with denominators at most $Q$ have circular distance at least $Q^{-2}$: subtracting two such fractions, or subtracting an integer from their difference, leaves a nonzero integer numerator over the product of their denominators. Apply the estimate with $\delta=Q^{-2}$. When there is just one fraction, the same arc argument applies directly. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-67"></a>
#### A degree-uniform plane-curve intersection bound

The appeals to Bézout’s theorem in Lemma <a href="#lem:nonlinear-points" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:nonlinear-points">30</a> require a bound uniform in both degrees and coefficients. Here is the needed affine form, proved by the elementary resultant argument. Only distinct points are counted; multiplicities are unnecessary.

<a id="stmt-lem-elementary-bezout"></a>
<div id="lem:elementary-bezout" class="lemma">

**Lemma 44**. *Let $F,G\in\mathbb C[X,Y]$ be nonzero polynomials of respective total degrees $m,n\ge1$, with no common nonconstant factor. Then they have at most $mn$ common zeros in $\mathbb C^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-elementary-bezout"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-elementary-bezout</summary>

*Proof.* First choose linear coordinates in which the coefficients of $Y^m$ in $F$ and $Y^n$ in $G$ are nonzero constants. Such coordinates exist: choose the direction of the $Y$-axis outside the zeros of the two highest homogeneous parts. Each part excludes only finitely many directions in the projective line. Scaling the polynomials makes these leading coefficients one.

Put $K=\mathbb C(X)$. The two polynomials are coprime in $K[Y]$. Indeed, clearing denominators in a common factor and removing common factors of its coefficients would give a common polynomial factor in $\mathbb C[X,Y]$. This is the elementary Gauss lemma: the product of two polynomials whose coefficients have no common divisor again has coefficients with no common divisor, as follows by reduction modulo any irreducible divisor in $\mathbb C[X]$.

Consider the linear map over $K$ <a id="eq:elementary-sylvester-map"></a>$$\label{eq:elementary-sylvester-map}
 (A,B)\longmapsto AF+BG,
 \qquad \deg_Y A<n,\quad\deg_Y B<m,$$ with target the polynomials of $Y$-degree less than $m+n$. It is injective: from $AF=-BG$ and coprimality, $F$ divides $B$, forcing $B=0$ and then $A=0$. Its matrix in monomial bases is the Sylvester matrix. Its determinant $R(X)$ is a nonzero polynomial. If $(x,y)$ is a common zero, every polynomial in the image of the specialized map at $X=x$ vanishes at $Y=y$. That image is a proper subspace, so $R(x)=0$. There are finitely many such $x$, and each fiber contains finitely many points because $F(x,Y)$ is monic of degree $m$. Thus the common zero set is finite.

Now choose linear coordinates once more so that the $X$-coordinates of those finitely many points are distinct and both leading coefficients in $Y$ remain nonzero constants. The first condition excludes the finitely many directions joining pairs of common zeros; the second excludes the directions on which either highest homogeneous part vanishes. A direction satisfying both exists. Repeat the construction of $R$ in these coordinates.

We claim that $\deg R\le mn$. Write $$F=\sum_{k=0}^{m}f_k(X)Y^k,\qquad
 G=\sum_{k=0}^{n}g_k(X)Y^k,
 \qquad \deg f_k\le m-k,\quad\deg g_k\le n-k.$$ View the rows of the Sylvester matrix as the coefficients of $Y^iF$ for $0\le i<n$ and $Y^jG$ for $0\le j<m$; label its columns by $Y^k$, $0\le k<m+n$. An entry from row $Y^iF$ and column $Y^k$ has degree at most $m+i-k$; an entry from row $Y^jG$ has degree at most $n+j-k$. Every nonzero term of the determinant uses each row and column once, so its degree is at most $$\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(m+i)+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(n+j)
       -\sum_{k=0}^{m+n-1}k=mn.$$ Every common zero gives a distinct root of the nonzero polynomial $R$. This proves the bound. ◻

</details>

For clarity, the coprimality conditions in Lemma <a href="#lem:nonlinear-points" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:nonlinear-points">30</a> can now be checked directly. An irreducible factor $Q$ of degree $d\ge2$ that is not proportional to a real polynomial is coprime to its coefficientwise conjugate. Both partial derivatives of $Q$ are nonzero: independence of one variable would force an irreducible polynomial over $\mathbb C$ to be linear. Their smaller degrees make them coprime to $Q$, giving at most $d(d-1)$ intersections for each derivative; a nonzero constant derivative has no common zeros with $Q$. The same lemma applies to the inflection polynomial <a href="#eq:inverse-inflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inverse-inflection">[eq:inverse-inflection]</a> when it is nonzero and not divisible by $Q$, since its degree is at most $3d-4$; a nonzero constant inflection polynomial has no zeros. If it is divisible by $Q$, every nonspecial smooth real graph arc has second derivative zero and lies on a line. Restricting $Q$ to that line would then give a univariate polynomial vanishing on an interval, hence identically; the line would be a factor of $Q$. This excludes such an arc and is exactly the alternative used in that proof.

<a id="pm-h-68"></a>
#### An order bound for primes in a progression

Brun–Titchmarsh gives a sharper constant than we need; see (Granville and Soundararajan 2014, Theorem 1.4.4) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[GranvilleSoundararajan2014](#ref-GranvilleSoundararajan2014)]. We derive the order bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:prime-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-inputs">4</a>(iii) from the fundamental lemma, in the form recorded in (Sofos 2023, Lemma 2.8) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Sofos2023](#ref-Sofos2023)], and Mertens’ formula. Working in progression coordinates keeps the remainder independent of the modulus.

<a id="stmt-lem-elementary-brun-titchmarsh"></a>
<div id="lem:elementary-brun-titchmarsh" class="lemma">

**Lemma 45**. *There are absolute constants $C,J_0>0$ such that, for every integer $q\ge1$, every integer $a$ with $(a,q)=1$, and every real $x$ and $H>0$ with $J=H/q\ge J_0$, $$\#\{x<p\le x+H:p\equiv a\pmod q\}
   \le C\frac{H}{\varphi(q)\log(H/q)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-elementary-brun-titchmarsh"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-elementary-brun-titchmarsh</summary>

<a id="eq:elementary-bt-sieve"></a>
*Proof.* Write the progression as $a+qj$, with $j$ in the real interval $$(x-a)/q<j\le(x+H-a)/q$$ of length $J$. Fix an absolute $s\ge s_0$ sufficiently large that $\theta=1/(2s)<1/2$, and put $v=J^\theta$ and $D=J^{1/2}=v^s$. For every prime $p\le v$ not dividing $q$, divisibility of $a+qj$ by $p$ specifies exactly one forbidden class of $j$ modulo $p$. For a squarefree product $l$ of these primes, the Chinese remainder theorem therefore gives the intersection count $$J/l+O(1),$$ with an absolute error independent of $x,a,q,l$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:fundamental" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fundamental">3</a> with $X=J$ and $g(p)=1/p$ on this prime set. If $N$ counts the surviving coordinates, it gives $$\label{eq:elementary-bt-sieve}
 N\ll J\prod_{\substack{p\le v\\p\nmid q}}(1-1/p)
       +D(1+\log D)^{C_2}$$ for an absolute constant $C_2$. To justify the remainder explicitly, let $C_1$ be the divisor-weight exponent in <a href="#eq:fundamental-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fundamental-input">[eq:fundamental-input]</a> and choose an integer $k\ge2^{C_1}$. On squarefree $l$, $\tau(l)^{C_1}\le\tau_k(l)$, where $\tau_k(l)$ counts ordered factorizations of $l$ into $k$ positive integers. Summing by the first $k-1$ factors gives $$\sum_{l\le D}\tau_k(l)
 \le D\left(\sum_{d\le D}\frac1d\right)^{k-1}
 \le D(1+\log D)^{k-1}.$$ In particular, the remainder in <a href="#eq:elementary-bt-sieve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:elementary-bt-sieve">[eq:elementary-bt-sieve]</a> has no dependence on $q$.

Mertens’ formula gives, for large $J$, $$\prod_{\substack{p\le v\\p\nmid q}}(1-1/p)
 =\prod_{p\le v}(1-1/p)
       \prod_{\substack{p\mid q\\p\le v}}(1-1/p)^{-1}
 \ll\frac1{\log v}\frac q{\varphi(q)}
 \ll\frac1{\log J}\frac q{\varphi(q)}.$$ Every prime in the progression that exceeds $v$ is among the survivors. The primes at most $v$ contribute at most $v$ further points, regardless of $q$. Thus the desired prime count is at most $$N+v\ll \frac{Jq}{\varphi(q)\log J}
          +J^{1/2}(1+\log J)^{C_2}+J^\theta.$$ The last two terms are $O(J/\log J)$ for all sufficiently large $J$, uniformly in $q$. Since $q/\varphi(q)\ge1$, they are absorbed by the first term. Substituting $J=H/q$ completes the proof. ◻

</details>

      "caption": "(154,98) (77,89)(0,0) (77,79)(0,-1)15 (81,71)(0,0)[l]$p$ (77,54)(0,0) (69,45)(-4,-1)43 (77,44)(0,-1)12 (85,45)(4,-1)43 (46,37)(0,0)$q$ (81,38)(0,0)[l]$q′$ (110,37)(0,0)$u$ (26,19)(0,0) (77,19)(0,0) (105,9.5) (46,19) Prospective child $dpu$ omitted by the admission rule (51,3)(0,0)$q, q′$ satisfy [eq:admission] (128,3)(0,0)$u$ fails [eq:admission] Admission and stopping have different roles. Only selected branches are drawn, with $q, q′, u 10.",
      "figure_id": "fig:admission-and-stopping",
      "pdf_page": 10,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:admission-and-stopping",
          "exact_quote": "Admission and stopping have different roles. Only selected branches\nare drawn, with $q,q',u<p$. At the upper node, admission is the test\n$r_{dp}-x(v)\\ge\\mathcal H(x(v))$ for the prospective prime $v$.\nThe omitted child $dpu$ does not belong",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "A tree diagram illustrating branch admission and stopping rules across three levels:\n- Top level: A solid rectangular node labeled:\n  \"Expanded lower node $d$\"\n  \"every available child is included\"\n- Edge from top to middle: A downward arrow labeled \"$p$\".\n- Middle level: A solid rectangular node labeled:\n  \"Included upper node $dp$\"\n  \"test each prospective lower child\"\n- Edges from middle to bottom: Three diverging downward arrows:\n  - Down-left arrow labeled \"$q$\"\n  - Downward arrow labeled \"$q'$\"\n  - Down-right arrow labeled \"$u$\"\n- Bottom level: Three nodes:\n  - Left node (solid rectangle):\n    \"Included lower node $dpq$\"\n    \"chosen stop: retain\"\n    \"$S_{dpq}(x(q))$ exactly\"\n  - Middle node (solid rectangle):\n    \"Included lower node $dpq'$\"\n    \"continue its\"\n    \"lower-bound expansion\"\n  - Right node (dashed rectangle):\n    \"Prospective child $dpu$\"\n    \"omitted by the\"\n    \"admission rule\"\n- Annotations below bottom level:\n  - Centered beneath the two solid nodes on the left: \"$q, q'$ satisfy (3.3)\"\n  - Centered beneath the dashed node on the right: \"$u$ fails (3.3)\""
    }
  ],
